# isomesh — BACKLOG ARCHIVE

Completed tickets, moved here from `BACKLOG.md` as they finished. **Never deleted.**

The annotations matter more than the checkmarks: several of these tickets were amended, split or
outright contradicted during implementation, and the reason is recorded on the row rather than lost to
the commit log. Where a ticket's premise turned out to be false, `FINDINGS.md` carries the falsified
entry and this file carries what the ticket did about it.

**Archived:** continuously since 2026-08-11. Rows are in completion order, not numeric order.

## Index

240 tickets. Line numbers are stable until something above them is edited — grep the ID if
they drift. **Read the annotation, not the checkmark**: the rows worth revisiting are the ones where
implementation contradicted the ticket.

| ☑ | **I-001** | **Workspace skeleton.** Root `Cargo.toml` with `members = ["crates/*"]`, `exclude = ["bevy_isomesh"]`. `crates/isomesh` with `libm 0.2` as its only dependency, edition 2024, `#![no_std]` + `extern crate alloc`, unconditionally. **Acceptance:** `cargo tree -p isomesh -e normal` prints exactly two packages. | S | — |
| | | *Amended during implementation.* Three changes, each with its reason. **(a)** `-e normal` added: cargo's default edges are `normal,build,dev`, so the bare command starts showing proptest's and criterion's trees the moment T-005 and T-006 land — it does not express the intended claim today either. **(b)** `glam` → `libm` as the sole dependency: `core` has no `sqrt`/`floor`/`sin`/`cos` on stable and `Real` needs all four, whereas nothing in Phase 0 needs vector types — fields are scalar and `validate.rs` wants one hand-written cross product. glam is still the sanctioned internal math library and lands at A-007. **(c)** The `std` default feature dropped: `libm` is used unconditionally so there is one float backend and one set of results, which also makes T-007's golden hashes portable across macOS and Linux. With nothing left for `std` to gate, keeping it would be a forward-declared stub. Justifications recorded in `CLAUDE.md`. | | |
| ☑ | **I-002** | **Core traits.** `Real` (sealed, f32/f64), `Sdf`, `Shape3`, `MeshSink`, `MeshBuffer`. Signatures per the brief — **plain arrays only, no glam in any public item.** `MeshBuffer::reset()` clears without shrinking. **Acceptance:** a test asserts capacity survives `reset()`; a test asserts `Sdf` is object-safe if you intend `dyn` use, or documents that it isn't. | M | I-001 |
| | | *One deviation.* `MeshSink` carries `type Scalar: Real` and `MeshBuffer` is `MeshBuffer<R: Real = f32>`, where the brief writes `[f32; 3]` literally. The brief also says twelve lines earlier that `fast-surface-nets`' `SignedDistance: Into<f32>` bound "forecloses f64" for CAD and not to repeat it — an f32-only sink repeats exactly that foreclosure at the output end, throwing an f64 field and an f64 QEF solve through f32 on the way out. The common spelling is unchanged (`MeshBuffer`), `dyn` use becomes `dyn MeshSink<Scalar = f32>`, and narrowing moves downstream to `bevy_isomesh` where GPU buffers are f32 anyway. Cheap now; it would break every public signature later. | | |
| ☑ | **I-003** | **Shared test fields** in `crates/isomesh/src/fields.rs`: `sphere`, `torus`, `box_exact`, `csg_difference` (box − sphere), `fbm_terrain`, `thin_plate`, `gyroid`. Each with an analytic gradient where one exists. **These are shared by tests, benches, and every Bevy example** — one definition, no drift. **Acceptance:** each field has a test asserting sign at a known inside and outside point. | M | I-002 |
| | | *Scope added, deliberately.* **(a)** A `ReferenceField` trait (`NAME`, `domain`, `closed_in_domain`, `expected_euler`, `is_exact_distance`). Without it the harness needs a per-field `if` ladder, because `gyroid` is periodic (a finite box cuts it, so it has boundary) and `fbm_terrain` is a heightfield (open by construction) — neither has an Euler characteristic assertable a priori, and inventing one would violate rule 5. **(b)** The canonical gyroid is capped to a sphere so it is closed at all, which needs an `Intersection` combinator alongside `Difference`. **(c)** `for_each_reference_field!` sweeps all seven with concrete types; `Vec<Box<dyn Sdf>>` would put a vtable on the innermost loop of every benchmark. | | |
| | | *Two numbers worth recording.* No document in this repo contains a single field formula, so all seven originate here, each cited in its doc comment. `fbm_terrain`'s noise is hand-rolled (rule 3 forbids a noise crate) and evaluates **no transcendental at all** — integer hash plus a polynomial — so it is bit-identical across platforms, which is what T-007's committed goldens need. Its amplitude bound is the **provable** `\|n\| ≤ 2` rather than the widely quoted `√3/2 ≈ 0.866`; measured max is **0.907716** over 43,200 samples, i.e. the folklore figure is *wrong for this implementation* and hard-coding it would have been a silent bug. | | |
| ☑ | **T-001** | **Mesh validity harness** — `crates/isomesh/src/validate.rs`, compiled in normal builds (consumers want it too). Reports, doesn't just assert: `euler_characteristic`, `non_manifold_edges` (edge used by ≠2 faces), `non_manifold_vertices`, `boundary_edges`, `degenerate_triangles`, `out_of_range_indices`, `duplicate_vertices`. Returns a `MeshReport` struct that `Display`s as one block. **Acceptance:** unit-tested against a hand-built tetrahedron (χ=2, 0 violations) and a deliberately broken mesh (each violation type detected). | M | I-002 |
| | | *Definitional choices, each a judgment call rather than a fact.* **(a)** `non_manifold_edges` counts ≥3 faces and `boundary_edges` exactly 1, splitting the ticket's literal "≠2" — lumping them together double-counts and makes zero unachievable for any open mesh, i.e. for `fbm_terrain` and for every individual chunk. **(b)** `V` in χ is *referenced* vertices, with `unreferenced_vertices` reported separately; otherwise a stale vertex pool reports χ=3 for a sphere and reads as a topology bug when it's an allocation bug. **(c)** `duplicate_vertices` is "has an earlier vertex within ε" — epsilon-closeness isn't transitive, so equivalence classes aren't well defined but this is. **(d)** `genus` is `Some` only for a single, consistently oriented, manifold component with no skipped faces; the orientation precondition is what makes `χ = 2−2g−b` sound. **(e)** No `Result`, no panic, no short-circuit: a `Result` forces a branch at every call site and `?` discards the partial report exactly when it's most useful. | | |
| | | *Three counters added beyond the list, each because its absence is a hole.* `inconsistently_oriented_edges` — a case table with one flipped triangle passes χ, edge-manifoldness *and* vertex-manifoldness while being inside out (fixture 10 demonstrates it). `boundary_loops` — `χ=1` alone can't distinguish a hole from a handle, and it's `b` not the edge count that enters `χ = 2−2g−b`. `non_finite_positions` — a NaN vertex quantises into one bucket, never matches as a duplicate, and `NaN <= threshold` is false so it isn't degenerate either; without this it passes silently. Also: `degenerate_triangles` is excluded from `violations()` and is a recorded metric, since Marching Cubes emits slivers for ordinary reasons. | | |
| ☑ | **T-002** | **Self-intersection counter.** Triangle–triangle intersection test, BVH or uniform grid broadphase. Reports intersections per 1,000 triangles. Doesn't need to be fast; needs to be right. **Acceptance:** two hand-placed crossing triangles detected; two adjacent triangles sharing an edge **not** counted (this is the trap). | M | T-001 |
| | | *Returns the intersecting **pairs**, not just a count.* The literature review is explicit that every detected pair must be bucketed intra-cell / inter-cell-within-chunk / cross-chunk-seam, since cross-seam hits are a stitching bug that would otherwise dominate λ. Chunks don't exist until G-001, so rather than invent a chunk concept now, the pairs come back sorted and bucketing becomes a caller's job the moment there's something to bucket by. Reports λ (per 1,000 triangles), never `p` — `p = 1 − e^{−λT}` saturates with chunk size. | | |
| | | *Two judgment calls.* **(a)** Pairs sharing **any** vertex index are excluded, not just edge-sharing ones. The ticket names the edge case, but excluding only that would report a false positive for every non-consecutive pair in every vertex fan, which swamps λ. Documented cost: a fold pinching exactly at a shared vertex isn't counted. **(b)** Tangential contact — meeting along a line or at a point — doesn't count; only overlap of positive measure does. Otherwise every correctly-stitched neighbour reads as a defect. Coplanar overlap *is* counted, via a separate 2D separating-axis path. | | |
| ☑ | **T-004** | **Determinism harness.** Run any extractor twice, assert byte-identical output buffers. **Acceptance:** wired into the test suite as a helper that every algorithm ticket calls. Catches `HashMap` iteration order leaking into vertex order — which it will. | S | T-001 |
| | | *"Byte-identical" is not `==`, and `==` is wrong in **both** directions:* `+0.0 == -0.0` is true though the bits differ (a sign flip on a zero coordinate is exactly what a reordered summation produces), and `NaN != NaN` is true though the bits may match. Comparison goes through `Real::total_cmp`, the IEEE `totalOrder` predicate, which compares full bit patterns. One fixture per direction. | | |
| | | *Three runs, not two.* Two fresh buffers catch non-determinism in the extractor; a third run into a **reused** buffer catches output that depends on the buffer's prior state. Every algorithm here is meant to be driven by resetting one buffer across thousands of chunks (rule 6) and nothing else in the suite drives one that way. | | |
| ☑ | **T-005a** | **Property-test scaffolding.** `proptest` generators for randomized fields (sphere unions, half-space convex bodies) and non-cubic grid sizes, plus `assert_extracted_mesh_is_valid` — the bundle every algorithm ticket runs on a mesh it produced. **Acceptance:** 1,000 cases green per property; every property has a negative control that makes it fail. | M | T-001, I-003 |
| | | *Split from T-005, because the original cannot be finished before the thing it tests exists.* Its second acceptance clause — "a deliberately corrupted **case table** makes it fail" — names A-001's deliverable, while A-001 was blocked on T-005: a cycle. What is buildable now is everything that does not need an extractor, and it is built. **Randomized fields have no known surface**, so the property checked is that they are **1-Lipschitz** (`min`/`max` of 1-Lipschitz functions are), which holds *everywhere* including the seams where a gradient check would have to skip. Negative controls: a field scaled by two must violate Lipschitz; a non-manifold mesh and a bad index must each stop the bundle. | | |
| ☑ | **I-004** | **CI.** GitHub Actions: fmt, clippy `-D warnings`, `cargo test` in the root workspace, **and `cd bevy_isomesh && cargo build --examples`**. That last one is the whole reason examples live where they do — `block-mesh`'s rotted to bevy 0.13 because nothing ever compiled them. **Acceptance:** CI green on a clean checkout. | S | I-001 |
| | | *The `bevy_isomesh` examples step is deferred to B-001*, which creates that crate — and B-001's row now carries it as an explicit sub-item, so it lives in the queue rather than in memory. Writing it now as a step that skips when the directory is missing would be a conditional that silently passes, which is precisely how the examples it exists to protect would rot. | | |
| | | *Three additions beyond the ticket.* **(a)** The test job runs on **ubuntu *and* macos**. The crate routes every transcendental through `libm` rather than `std` specifically so results are bit-identical across platforms and T-007's committed goldens are portable; running both is what turns that from a claim into a checked one. **(b)** `RUSTDOCFLAGS=-D warnings cargo doc` — clippy's `missing_docs` covers doc *presence*, nothing covered links pointing at nothing. **(c)** An MSRV job that reads `rust-version` out of `cargo metadata` and builds with exactly that toolchain. `1.85` was an unverified claim to consumers; it is now checked, and was confirmed locally against a real 1.85.1 install before this landed. | | |
| ☑ | **A-001** | **Marching Cubes.** Standard 256-case table. **Also write the structural validator** described in the brief: for all 256 configurations, generated triangles' boundary edges must lie on cube faces and be consistent with that face's corner signs. Cite the table's source in a comment. Document the sign convention (**negative = inside**) and winding order on the public fn. | L | T-001, T-005a |
| | | *The table is **derived at compile time**, not transcribed.* home-still holds the right papers — Lewiner et al. 2003 (`10.1080/10867651.2003.10487582`) and Lengyel's 2010 dissertation — but **their case tables did not survive PDF→text conversion**: Lengyel's are figures of cube diagrams, Lewiner's Table 1 converts to scrambled cells. Reading triangulations off diagrams is the guessing rule 5 forbids. So the construction walks each face's corners CCW-from-outside; every cut edge gets exactly one entry and one exit (two faces induce opposite directions on a shared edge), the segments close into directed cycles with nothing left over, and each cycle is fanned. Lengyel §3.1.1 supplies the structure, the sign convention and the zero-sample rule — cited, and quoted in the module docs. | | |
| | | *Cross-checked against the published table anyway.* Bourke's listing was fetched with `curl` and parsed by regex — no language model ever touched a digit — and is committed as a test fixture with its URL and SHA-256. The test **derives** the correspondence rather than assuming it: Bourke's edge→corner pairs are recovered from the XOR structure of his `edgeTable`, then the cube relabelling is found by search. Result: **all 256 cases produce the identical surface, 0 disagreements**, with winding reversed — and `a_single_inside_corner_faces_outward` plus a signed-volume check establish that ours is the outward one. | | |
| | | *Measured, at 49³ unless noted:* sphere 2312 tris (33³) χ=2 · torus 4848 χ=0 genus 1 · box_exact 2696 (33³) χ=2 · csg_difference 4484 (41³) χ=2 · **capped gyroid 24108 tris, χ=−24, genus 13** (the value `expected_euler()` correctly declines to predict) · fbm_terrain 3946 (33³) χ=1, open. Self-intersections **0.000 per 1,000** on a sphere — the baseline A-009 compares the Dual Contouring clamp against. **A grid sample landing exactly on the isosurface produces slivers**: at 25³ over `[-2,2]`, 30 lattice points sit exactly on the unit sphere, giving 96 degenerate triangles and 2.301 intersecting pairs per 1,000 — while χ stays 2. That is sampling, not extraction, and is why degenerates are a recorded metric rather than a gate. | | |
| ☑ | **A-004** | **Surface Nets.** One vertex per sign-changing cell at the centroid of edge crossings, quad or triangle output. Optional Laplacian smoothing passes as a parameter. **Note for the record:** this has no credible published timings anywhere despite being what engines ship — your benchmark will be the reference. | M | T-001 |
| | | **Two claims in the brief and the folklore are measurably false, and this is the ticket that disproved them.** *(a)* The brief expects Surface Nets and Marching Cubes triangle counts "to differ substantially". They differ by **exactly 2χ** — 4 on a sphere, 0 on a torus, 4 on box_exact, 4 on csg_difference, exact on all four. That is forced by Euler: Marching Cubes places one vertex per crossed grid edge, Surface Nets emits two triangles per crossed grid edge, and any closed triangulated surface has `F = 2V − 2χ`, so `F_sn = F_mc + 2χ`. Surface Nets is **not** the cheaper method by triangle count. *(b)* "Every interior vertex has four neighbours" is false once triangulated — measured max degree 10 vs Marching Cubes' 9. What Surface Nets actually buys is quad connectivity and one vertex per *cell* rather than per *edge*. | | |
| | | *And the structural limit, pinned as a measurement.* One vertex per cell means two sheets through one cell are forced to share it, so the output is **non-manifold** exactly where the literature review says it will be: **48 non-manifold edges on the capped gyroid at 49³, 15 on fbm_terrain at 33³** (its highest octave has a wavelength near the cell size, so the terrain folds inside single cells). Orientation and boundary stay clean, so the damage is purely connectivity — "Dual Contouring's actual structural defect", fixed architecturally by A-010's vertex splitting rather than by patching. The test asserts these are non-zero, so the numbers move only when someone means them to. Rounded corners measured too: nearest vertex to `box_exact`'s corner is **1.15 cells** away. | | |
| ☑ | **B-001** | **Crate skeleton.** Own workspace (excluded from root). Depends on **leaf crates** — `bevy_app`, `bevy_ecs`, `bevy_asset`, `bevy_mesh` — with `bevy_render` **optional** behind a `gpu` feature, so a CPU-only consumer never compiles the renderer. Pin `bevy 0.19`. | S | I-002 |

CI runs `cargo check --all-targets` in this workspace rather than `build --examples`: it type-checks every example, which is what catches the API rot that pinned `block-mesh` to bevy 0.13, without paying to codegen and link a full Bevy app on every push. *Verified on resolve:* glam **0.32.1**, encase **0.12.0**, wgpu-types **29.0.4** — matching CLAUDE.md's pins. No `x11` (that is Linux; this targets macOS/Metal). | | |

| ☑ | **B-004** | **`examples/common/`.** Orbit camera, HUD (rolling 30-frame medians, not instantaneous), universal keybindings (`W` wire, `N` normals, `G` grid, `Space` pause, `R` remesh, `F12` screenshot, `Esc` quit), field picker. Build this **before** any example — it's what makes 27 examples cheap. | M | B-002 |
| ☑ | **E-101** | `marching_cubes_sphere` — Marching Cubes baseline, wireframe toggle | A-001, B-004 |
| | | *Runs, and was verified by running it — not by type-checking it.* Screenshots committed to `docs/screenshots/`. The HUD reports the **validity harness live on the mesh being rendered**: 1158 verts, 2312 tris (matching A-001's committed measurement), χ=2, 0 non-manifold edges, 0 boundary edges, MANIFOLD CLOSED, 0.158 ms extract at 60 fps. `[`/`]` change resolution live. | |
| ☑ | **E-103** | `surface_nets_vs_marching_cubes` — Surface Nets next to Marching Cubes, triangle counts | A-004 |
| | | *Renamed and re-scoped, because its premise was disproved by A-004.* It is not sphere-only (four switchable fields) and the triangle counts do **not** differ substantially — the HUD now shows `F_sn − F_mc = 2χ` recomputed live, so you can watch the identity hold as field and resolution change. Screenshots in `docs/screenshots/`. | |
| | | *A trap found while writing it, worth knowing before anyone benchmarks sharp features.* **`box_exact` is exactly zero across its entire boundary** — `f(1,0,0)`, `f(1,1,0)` and `f(1,1,1)` are all `+0` — so a grid plane lying on a box face classifies wholly as *outside* and the sign change moves a cell inward. Consequence: on a grid-aligned box **Marching Cubes lands *further* from the corner than Surface Nets does** (1.41 cells vs 1.15 at 25³), the opposite of the intuition that edge-placed vertices can hit corners. Over the ±2 domain, 25 and 33 samples are grid-aligned and 27 is not (0.71 vs 0.58 cells). **E-104 must use a box that is not grid-aligned**, or it measures the zero-classification rule rather than the algorithm. | |
| ☑ | **A-006** | **Hermite data extraction.** Per sign-changing edge: crossing position (bisection or analytic) **and surface normal there**. `HermiteCell` holding up to 12 crossings. This is Dual Contouring's real input and the reason Dual Contouring can do sharp features at all. **Acceptance:** `hermite_debug` gizmo data is correct on `box_exact` — normals on a flat face are identical, normals across an edge are not. | M | I-003 |
| ☑ | **T-003** | **Accuracy harness.** One-sided and symmetric Hausdorff distance from the mesh to an analytic SDF, sampled on triangle centroids and vertices. Also mean absolute error. **Acceptance:** a unit sphere meshed at 64³ has max error below one cell diagonal. | M | I-003 |
| | | *"64³" means 64 **samples**, so `h = (hi−lo)/63 = 4/63 = 0.063492` and the cell diagonal is `0.10997148`. Recorded because the repo carries a second, conflicting reading: `ThinPlate::CANONICAL_CELL_SIZE = 2·COMPACT_DOMAIN/64` divides by 64 rather than 63. The `mesh()` helper in `marching_cubes/tests.rs` and the SAMPLES-not-cells convention govern, and the difference is 1.6% — enough to matter if someone later commits a golden threshold. | |
| | | *Distance is measured geometrically, not from `\|f(p)\|`, and this is one unconditional path rather than two.* `\|f\|` is a distance only where `\|∇f\| = 1`, which is false for `gyroid` and `fbm_terrain`, so branching on `is_exact_distance()` would put two execution paths in the harness. The obvious branch-free repair `\|f\|/\|∇f\|` is rejected by the field module's own argument — dividing by `\|∇f\|` "introduces a second failure mode wherever `\|∇g\| → 0`" — and is only first order. What ships is the **gradient-flow chord**: Newton-project the sample onto the zero set and measure how far it moved. Exact for the five true distance fields (the first step lands on the surface and its length *is* `\|f\|`), convergent on the other two, and it follows `fields/mod.rs`'s standing instruction that "an accuracy harness should measure against geometry rather than against `\|sample\|`". The bias is an over-estimate, which makes a "below X" gate conservative. | |
| | | *The reverse direction earns its cost, and there is a test that proves it.* Deleting one face of an inscribed octahedron leaves `mesh_to_field` **bit-identical** and moves `field_to_mesh` to `√(3/2 − 2/√3)`. Forward sampling sees misplaced geometry; only reverse sampling sees missing geometry. On real fields it is the reverse number that finds Marching Cubes rounding `box_exact`'s corner (0.1443 against 0.0833 forward) and an under-resolved `thin_plate` (0.0893 against 0.0083). Seeds come from the extractor's own lattice, band-filtered by the first Newton step — deliberately not from sign-changing cells, because a sub-voxel `thin_plate` produces no sign change at all and sign-based seeding would be blind exactly where the mesh is missing. | |
| | | *The stated acceptance criterion is 80× loose and is not what constrains the code.* Marching Cubes measures `1.380e-3` against a `0.10997` threshold, so a harness returning a constant would pass. Shipped anyway, with the tests that do constrain it: two octahedra whose every quantity is closed-form and which fail *separately* if either sample population is dropped (inscribed → vertices contribute 0, circumscribed → centroids contribute 0); a triangle mesh lying exactly on a box face, asserted bit-equal to `+0.0`; the spatial index checked bit-for-bit against brute force at three spacings; and a convergence-order test measuring `h²` falloff at **4.179** against an ideal 4.13. | |
| | | *Three numbers worth keeping.* Surface Nets is **1.66×** less accurate than Marching Cubes at 64³ on both max and mean, despite ✗1 showing it emits `2χ` more triangles. Surface cells are **`1.5·A/h²`**, not `A/h²` — the constant is `E[\|nₓ\|+\|n_y\|+\|n_z\|] = 3/2` over the unit sphere, and estimating without it under-predicted the 64³ triangle count by 1.47×. The whole 181-test suite runs in **5.5 s** in a debug build, so no second code path was needed to keep the harness affordable. | |

Without it a corrupted-table failure would be indistinguishable from the double having drifted, and the whole check would prove nothing. | |

Plus a control asserting the *uncorrupted* table passes the same path, without which all three would also pass if the double emitted garbage unconditionally. | |

The corruption is now confined to edges the case actually cuts, which is both the plausible transcription error and the one that reaches the gate. | | | *Grids are constructed so "closed" is an assertion rather than a hope:* the spacing comes from the **shortest** axis, so every axis spans at least the full generator domain and longer axes simply extend into empty space. Resolution starts at 6 rather than T-005a's 2 — a one-cell axis puts every corner outside the object and returns an empty mesh, which is valid and proves nothing. | | | *Both of the literature's branches fire, on disjoint fields, which is a sharper result than either alone.* The review states the decision rule **in advance**: λ→0 means the failures were placement; λ unchanged means they are *connectivity* and the fix is architectural. | ☑ | **T-007** | **Golden-hash regression.** Stable hash of (positions, normals, indices) for each (algorithm, field, resolution). | | | *It answers the one question nothing else in the suite can: **did the output change at all?*** Every other check tests a property. The validity harness checks topology and would shrug at a vertex that moved; the accuracy harness checks distance to the surface and would shrug at one that moved by an ulp; the property suite generates fresh meshes each run and has no memory. A change that is topologically identical, geometrically indistinguishable and statistically invisible — a reordered summation, a different rounding on one axis — passes all of them and reaches a consumer as a silent diff. 63 combinations: 7 fields × 3 algorithms × 3 resolutions. |
| | | *FNV-1a, hand-rolled, ten lines.* `std`'s `DefaultHasher` is **documented as unstable across Rust releases**, so a fixture built on it would break on a toolchain bump while claiming the mesher had changed — the one thing a regression fixture must never lie about. The hash consumes raw IEEE bit patterns rather than formatted decimals, so it separates `+0.0` from `-0.0`, which is exactly what a reordered summation produces and precisely the class T-004 exists to catch. Counts are hashed first so a truncated buffer cannot collide with a prefix of the whole. |

| | | *Shipped as `ChunkId` + `ChunkLayout` rather than `Chunk<T>`.* Nothing here owns sample data: a chunk is a *coordinate system*, and the payload it would wrap differs per consumer — a game holds a fixed-point slab, a CAD tool holds an expression tree. `sample_shape()` returns `cells + 1` per axis, which is the overlap, and hands straight to any extractor because it counts samples in exactly the convention they already use. |
| | | *Every world position is derived from an exact **global integer** sample index, through one function.* That is the whole trick: `sample_origin(id) = world_of_sample(base_sample(id))`, with `base_sample` in `i64`. Deriving a chunk's origin by floating-point accumulation instead is how two chunks end up disagreeing about where their shared plane is. |
| | | *The acceptance criterion passes exactly — and only for power-of-two cell sizes, which is a finding rather than a caveat (M-32).* At `h = 0.125`, **16 of 16** shared-plane vertices are bit-identical between independently meshed chunks. At `h = 4/35`, **0 of 14** are, with a worst gap of `1.57e-16` world units. The cause is that an extractor computes `origin + h·local`, so chunk `c`'s last plane is `(o + h·cn) + h·n` while `c+1`'s first is `o + h·(c+1)n` — equal by algebra, not by IEEE. A search over 200,000 random `(origin, h, cells, chunk)` combinations found **22% disagree**. It is a rounding error, not a crack, but it decides whether a chunked world inherits the bit-identity T-007 and M-31 establish for a single volume. |
| | | *The non-power-of-two test first passed while proving nothing,* which earned its own method rule. `h = 4/33` *looks* irregular and happens to land in the 78% that agree exactly, so the test named after the disagreeing case was measuring the agreeing one. The fixture now comes from the search, and asserts up front that the two expressions still differ — a test that can only pass is the same defect as one that cannot fail. |

The finding wrote that down before this module existed — *"expect the assertion to fail there and do not 'fix' it"* — and the test asserts the boundary is non-empty, because a chunk with no boundary would mean the seam was not being cut at all. |
| | | *Ownership is half-open on the positive side*, matching where the overlap sits, so each sample plane has exactly one owner and a point exactly on a seam is never claimed twice. `local_sample` uses `div_euclid`/`rem_euclid` so negative chunk coordinates floor rather than truncate toward zero — round-tripped over a 5³ block of chunks including negatives. |

Unpublished number; log it. | M | G-001 |
| | | ***E1, measured: a brush changes 15–36% of the cells in its own bounding box.*** Sphere brush carved two cells deeper at `h = 0.0625` — radius `0.25` → **36.0%**, `0.50` → **29.2%**, `0.75` → **24.4%**, `1.00` → **14.9%**. It falls as roughly `1/r` for a geometric reason: the changed set is a shell of fixed thickness growing as `r²`, inside a bounding box growing as `r³`. **The incremental case is real but not overwhelming** — a bounding-box re-mesh does 3–7× the necessary work, not 30×. |
| | | *The first version of this number was 100%, and it was wrong in the direction that would have killed the feature (M-34).* Counting cells whose *samples* moved reads 100% at radius `0.25`, which says incremental meshing buys nothing. But an SDF brush perturbs values throughout its support — carving a sphere deeper moves the field everywhere the sphere term dominates, deep inside the solid included — and a cell wholly inside or wholly outside emits no triangles either way. Counting cells whose **triangles** move gives 36%. The overstatement is 2.8–3.7×, and the method rule is: count what changes in the output, not what changes in the input. |
| | | *A sign change does **not** imply an output change, which looks obviously false (M-35).* A cell can go from wholly outside to wholly inside in one edit: every corner flips, and it is a surface cell neither before nor after, so it emits nothing either side and needs no re-mesh. Measured **167 of 1155** such cells at radius `0.25` and **1040 of 8660** at `1.00`. They are reported as `swept_cells` — the signature of an edit moving further than one cell per step, which is where a stroke starts skipping geometry between frames. The assertion that caught this was the one written expecting containment. |
| | | *Chunk granularity is measured rather than guessed:* the same edit marks **60.4%** of chunks at 8 cells/chunk and **100%** at 16 and 32, against a cell-level 29.2%. The cell fraction is a property of the edit; the chunk fraction is a property of the chunk size, and the gap is what a finer dirty granularity would buy. |
| | | *`DirtySet` is sorted and deduplicated on insert*, so iteration order is a function of the set's contents rather than of the order edits arrived — a re-mesh queue that depends on insertion order gives different output for the same world. `mesh_dirty` hands the caller each chunk's origin from `ChunkLayout::sample_origin` rather than letting them compute it, so the seam arithmetic of M-32 cannot be got wrong at the call site, and clears the queue only after the last chunk so a panic mid-way loses no work. |
| | | *Nothing owns sample data,* for the same reason `ChunkLayout` does not: an edit is expressed as *two fields*, before and after, which the crate's field-first design already provides. A consumer holding a fixed-point slab and one holding an expression tree both produce a before and an after, and neither adopts a storage type to be measured. |
| ☑ | **G-003** | **Brush operations.** Add/subtract sphere, box, capsule against the field. `min`/`max` for union/difference, smooth-min variant. Fixed-point (`i32`) storage option for bit-exact determinism. **Acceptance:** a determinism test — 8 brush ops applied in all 40,320 orderings; count distinct results. Expect 1; if it's not 1, you've lost commutativity and the multiplayer story dies here. | M | G-001 |
| | | ***The acceptance criterion passes, and the ticket's "expect 1" turns out to be true of exactly one case in three.*** All eight `Add` over all 40,320 orderings give **1** distinct result, bit-identical; all eight `Subtract` likewise. `min` and `max` are commutative *and* associative in IEEE and introduce no rounding at all — they select an argument rather than computing a value. **The multiplayer story survives**: concurrent clients may reorder a run of same-kind hard edits freely and converge bit for bit. |
| | | *Two cases where it is emphatically not 1, and they fail for different reasons.* **Mixed add and subtract: 11 distinct results.** Carving a hole then filling it is a different solid from filling then carving — semantic, not numerical, and no storage format repairs it. **Eight smooth adds: 40,317 of 40,320 distinct**, because smooth-min is not associative. So "all the same operation" is not sufficient for reordering; only "all the same *hard* operation" is, and `BrushOp::commutes_with` returns that honest answer rather than the optimistic one. |
| | | *The module docs claimed smooth-min was commutative until a test disagreed (M-38).* It is commutative *algebraically* — swapping `a` and `b` maps `h` to `1 − h` and leaves both terms invariant on paper — but the swap evaluates a different expression and the two agree only to rounding: **1 ulp** apart, measured. That is a separate failure from non-associativity, which is **`1.694e-2`**, four orders larger. They have different fixes, which is why the entry separates them. |
| | | *The associativity fixture had to be found by search, which is G-001's method rule arriving a second time.* The first attempt used values `0.5` apart with `k = 0.4` — but once `|a − b| ≥ k` the blend saturates, the `k·h·(1−h)` term vanishes and smooth-min degenerates to an ordinary `min`, which **is** associative. The test asserting non-associativity was exercising the associative case and failing for the right reason. Two occurrences in two tickets is a rule, not an anecdote. |
| | | ***Fixed-point storage is not implemented, on the evidence (M-39).*** The ticket proposes an `i32` option "for bit-exact determinism", but `min` and `max` are already exact in IEEE and the ordering guarantee holds in `f64` with nothing added — the acceptance criterion passes without it. The only place fixed-point would help is smooth-min's 1-ulp commutativity gap, which is the *lesser* of that operation's two reordering failures and does not rescue it. `Real` is a sealed trait, so adding a fixed-point scalar is a large change to buy a property the crate already has. |
| | | *`Capsule` joins `Sphere` and `BoxExact` as the third brush shape* — the classic point-to-segment distance, verified as an exact distance field by central difference (`|∇f| = 1` to `1e-6`), with a zero-length capsule degenerating to a sphere rather than being rejected. A brush that is not an exact distance field distorts whatever it is combined into. |
| | | *Edits compose rather than mutate:* `BrushStack` is an `Sdf` wrapping a base and a slice of brushes, which is exactly what lets G-002 express an edit as *two fields* and measure what moved. |
| ☑ | **A-002** | **Marching Cubes 33 / asymptotic decider.** Face saddle `S = (f00·f11 − f10·f01) / (f00 + f11 − f10 − f01)`, guard the denominator. Interior ambiguity via the trilinear body saddle. **Re-scoped — the original acceptance was unsatisfiable, see ✗11.** A-001 cannot hole: `validate_table()` checks all 256 cases and `marching_cubes/tests.rs:30` asserts `face_disagreements == 0`, because a face's segments are a function of that face's own four corner signs. So no field holes under A-001 and "closed under A-002" can never be demonstrated. What Marching Cubes 33 actually buys is **topological agreement with the trilinear interpolant**, not crack-fixing. **Acceptance:** a cell configuration with an ambiguous face on which A-001 and A-002 report **different χ**, with A-002 matching the trilinear connectivity — a test where both agree isn't testing this. Spend the `L` knowing the game-facing value is consistency, not hole-closing. | L | A-001 |
| | | ***The acceptance criterion passes, decisively: 88 of the 256 cases change χ.*** The smallest is case 6 — corners 1 and 2 inside, diagonally opposite on the `z = 0` face and on no other face together, so exactly one ambiguous face. Separated, that face cuts off each corner with its own triangle: two discs, `χ = 2`. Joined, they become one disc, `χ = 1`. Searched over all 256 cases rather than picked, and pinned in both directions. |
| | | *Shipped as a setter, not a type.* `MarchingCubes::set_face_ambiguity(FaceAmbiguity::Separate \| ::AsymptoticDecider)`, defaulting to `Separate`, matching `dc::set_clamp(Clamp)`'s precedent. Marching Cubes and Marching Cubes 33 differ in **one thing only** — how an ambiguous face pairs its four cut edges — so one extract loop means they cannot drift. `segment_links(case, joined)` now takes a six-bit mask, `triangulate()` is split out of the table construction, and `CASES` is defined as `triangulate(segment_links(case, 0))` so the compile-time table and the runtime decider path are literally the same two functions. |
| | | ***No division, no epsilon, and the brief's "guard the denominator" is unnecessary.*** On an ambiguous face one diagonal is strictly negative and the other non-negative — a sample of exactly zero is outside — so the denominator `v0+v2−v1−v3` is strictly non-zero *by the sign rule alone*. Only `sign(S)` is wanted and the denominator's sign is known, so the test collapses to comparing the two diagonal products: **joined ⟺ `d_in > d_out`**. Both branches of the derivation give the same comparison. Same shape of argument as `edge_crossing`'s missing epsilon. |
| | | *The crack-free property is preserved and is now a proof rather than a hope.* Two cells meeting on a face list its corners in orders differing by a rotation and a reflection; neither changes which diagonal is which, and IEEE multiplication is commutative and correctly rounded, so `v0·v2` and `v2·v0` are the same bits. `the_decider_is_invariant_under_rotation_and_reflection` checks all four rotations × two reflections over a systematic ladder of values. `validate_decider_table()` extends A-001's structural sweep to all **16,384** (case, mask) pairs, with the face-locality memo re-keyed on the decision bit; zero disagreements. |
| | | ***Verified against the interpolant it claims to read, by dense sampling.*** `the_decider_agrees_with_the_bilinear_interpolant` samples `B(s,t)` on a 257² grid, flood-fills the negative region and asks whether the two inside corners land in one component — no formula shared with the implementation, so an algebra error cannot hide in both. Near-degenerate saddles are skipped, because the neck between the hyperbola branches is `~2·sqrt(\|S/a\|)` wide and a finite grid cannot see it below its own spacing; the skip count is asserted to stay a small fraction so the test cannot go vacuous. |
| | | ***The measured cost of the rule is that it almost never fires (M-40).*** Custodio et al. report that real data is dominated by the unambiguous configurations; measured here at 33³ that is an understatement. On **sphere, torus, box_exact, csg_difference and thin_plate an ambiguous face never occurs at all** — 0 of 1160, 1128, 1352, 1388 and 512 surface cells — so Marching Cubes 33 and Marching Cubes are **bit-identical** on five of the seven reference fields, which the golden fixture now pins. Only `gyroid` (27 of 5240, 0.515%) and `fbm_terrain` (30 of 1958, 1.532%) reach it, and the decider joins about half the ambiguous faces it finds. 136 of the 256 cases have no ambiguous face at all. |
| | | ***A prediction that was wrong, and the finding it produced (✗17).*** The plan predicted zero non-manifold edges, on the grounds that Custodio's non-manifold mechanism needs tunnels and tunnels need the interior test. The gyroid at 25³ came back with **2 non-manifold edges against plain Marching Cubes' 0**. Inspected rather than explained away: both offending mesh edges carry **4** faces, both join two cut cube edges that lie on a shared cube face but are *not* connected by a segment there, and both are produced twice by each of the two adjacent cells — i.e. they are **fan chords**, not a topology error. An exhaustive two-cell search then showed plain Marching Cubes does exactly the same thing at exactly the same rate: **12 of 4,096 sign patterns, worst case 4 faces, identical under both rules.** The defect belongs to A-001's fan, not to the decider, and it is ticketed as **A-015**. |
| | | *So `is_closed()` is deliberately not the gate in the decider's reference-field sweep, and the reason is recorded on the test.* `is_closed()` folds in manifoldness, manifoldness is owned by the fan, and asserting it there would attribute A-001's problem to A-002. What the sweep does assert is everything the ambiguity rule is responsible for — crack-freeness on closed fields, consistent orientation, and the published χ. Following M-16, no even-χ parity check is made alongside a waived manifoldness, since parity is a corollary of it. The manifold census is pinned separately and exactly: `[("gyroid", 25, 2, 0)]`, failing if it spreads *and* if it disappears. |
| | | ***The "~free" claim now has a benchmark behind it (M-42).*** The v1 catalog prices the asymptotic decider at *"~free"* against the guaranteed version's 730 subcases; rule 4 forbids repeating that without the measurement. Median extraction, f32, Apple M5: `sphere` 33³ **206.25 → 205.65 µs** (−0.3%, noise), `sphere` 65³ **1.4954 → 1.5189 ms** (+1.6%), `gyroid` 33³ **786.89 → 795.44 µs** (+1.1%), `gyroid` 65³ **5.6378 → 5.8236 ms** (+3.3%). `sphere` has no ambiguous face at all, so its difference is the price of *asking*; `gyroid`'s extra ~1.7 points is the price of *answering*. |
| | | ***The decider does not widen M-32's chunk-seam problem (M-44), and the first attempt to measure that proved nothing.*** M-32 found two adjacent chunks compute a shared sample plane differently by an ulp; the decider turns sample values into a *discrete* choice, so a flip there is a crack rather than a shifted vertex. Over 217 divergent seam planes and 499,968 faces lying in them: **0** sign disagreements, 205 ambiguous faces, **0** decision flips, and the closest any came to its own boundary was a relative margin of **1.535e-2** — fourteen orders above the perturbation. The first sweep returned **0 ambiguous seam faces**, i.e. a pass that measured nothing; the test's own `ambiguous_faces > 100` reachability gate caught it and forced a retune. Third occurrence of the fixture trap, and the first caught by a test rather than a reviewer. |
| | | *Recorded numbers.* The decider's worst case is **10 triangles** per cell against the separated table's 5 — predicted before running, from the longest possible cycle using all twelve cut edges. `MAX_TRIANGLES` was already 12 and did not move. Self-intersections on the gyroid at 33³ under the decider: **0.0000 per 1,000 triangles**. Determinism passes on all three runs including the reused-buffer one. The golden fixture grows from 63 rows to **84**. |
| | | ***The interior test is not implemented, on evidence, and is ticketed as A-002b*** — the same shape of decision as G-003's M-39. `catalog-v2.md:107` says to skip it outright for a game; Custodio et al. prove Chernyaev's version is wrong for case 13.5 and that Lewiner's implementation omits cases 10 and 12, so there is no correct published table to transcribe and rule 5 forbids inventing one; and the v1 catalog prices the difference as *"~free"* against **730 subcases**. |
| ☑ | **A-015** | **Chord-free cycle triangulation.** The fan in `marching_cubes/table.rs`'s `triangulate` picks an interior diagonal of the cycle, and *two adjacent cells can pick the same one* — putting four triangles on one mesh edge. Measured exhaustively at A-002: **12 of 4,096 two-cell sign patterns, identically under A-001's separated rule and A-002's decider**, so this is Marching Cubes' defect and not the ambiguity rule's. It is the second mechanism behind ✗15, which attributed Marching Cubes' non-manifoldness entirely to sub-cell pinching. Any triangulation of a `k`-gon that adds no vertices has `k−3` chords, so the fix needs a per-cycle centroid vertex — which **breaks ✗1/M-2/M-22's `V_mc = C` identity and re-baselines every golden hash**. That cost is why this is its own ticket. **Acceptance:** `the_fan_lets_adjacent_cells_share_an_interior_chord` reports a worst case of 2 faces, and the census in `the_decider_non_manifold_census_is_pinned` is empty. | M | A-001 |
| | | ***The naive fix works and costs +73% of the mesh; the ticket shipped the other one.*** A centroid for every cycle of four or more removes the collision — the exhaustive two-cell search drops from 12 affected patterns to 0 — but measured across the seven reference fields at three resolutions it costs **23,034 → 39,881 vertices and 45,662 → 79,356 triangles**, `+73.1%` and `+73.8%`, worst on `box_exact` at `+99.7%`. Almost every cycle is long enough to qualify, so "a vertex and two triangles per long cycle" is a per-cycle truth and a whole-mesh disaster. |
| | | ***The cheap fix comes from asking which chords can actually collide.*** Only a cell containing **both** of a chord's cut edges can emit that mesh edge, and two cells share a pair of cube edges only when the edges share a cube **face** — two cells that meet along an edge or at a corner share no pair. So a fan is safe when no chord of it joins two edges of one face, which is local and `const`-evaluable (`cube::edges_share_a_face`). `safe_apex` returns the first vertex with that property. **Measured over all 256 cases and every canonical resolution mask: a safe apex exists for every cycle of length 3–7 and for 48 of the 60 length-8 cycles; only lengths 9 and 12 have none.** Plain Marching Cubes tops out at length 7, so it never reaches the centroid path, and **✗1/M-2/M-22's `V_mc = C` is intact** — the re-baselining this ticket was written to pay for never happened. |
| | | *Final cost across the whole 84-row golden fixture: **one row's counts moved**.* `marching_cubes+decider/gyroid/25`, `2940 → 2946` vertices and `5916 → 5928` triangles. Twelve rows changed hash, all `mc` or `mc33` on `gyroid` or `fbm_terrain`, and eleven of those are the same vertices and triangles fanned from a different apex. |
| | | ***✗15's mechanism was wrong, and this is what proved it (see the amended entry).*** The union of three spheres at `h = 2/3` — the fixture that falsified "Marching Cubes is unconditionally manifold" — now reports **0 non-manifold edges and 0 non-manifold vertices**. A triangulation cannot repair two sheets genuinely meeting inside a cell, so the cause was never the sub-cell pinch it was attributed to. Its `χ` was wrong too: a collided mesh edge is counted once in `E` while carrying four faces, so `E` was short by exactly the two collisions and `χ` long by two. The same fixture now reads `χ = 0`, genus 1 — a real handle at that spacing. |
| | | *So the property suite's Marching Cubes gate goes back to the strict `SurfaceGate::Closed` it was waived from at ✗15, and the decider's with it.* **8,000 generated cases pass**, where before A-015 the strict gate failed on the first fresh seed. That is evidence and not proof, and it is recorded as such: **O-12** asks whether Marching Cubes is now unconditionally manifold, and a future CI failure on a fresh seed would be the first example of a second mechanism rather than a regression. |
| | | *One regression caught by benchmarking rather than by tests.* The first implementation moved the edge-position computation **above** the vertex-cache check so a cycle centroid could average positions — which made every cache hit recompute a divide and a lerp, and a grid edge is asked for by up to four cells. Plain Marching Cubes, whose output is byte-identical, came back **~18% slower**. Split into `edge_position` (the one definition of where a crossing sits) called by the cache miss and by the centroid pre-pass, so the hot path keeps its early return. After the split, `decider/marching_cubes/sphere/f32/33` measures **205.77 µs against the 206.25 µs A-002 baseline** — the same bench, the same machine, both with intervals under 1% — so the fix is free on the path that never uses it. The 65³ pairs were not stable enough on this machine to quote and are not. |
| | | *`MAX_TRIANGLES` did not move and is now reached.* The separated table's worst case is still **5**, unchanged, because plain Marching Cubes never fans from a centroid. The decider's rose from 10 to **12** — exactly the two triangles a centroid fan costs over a vertex fan on the twelve-edge cycle, which is the one that has no safe apex. |

| ☑ | **A-013** | **Vertex welding and dedup.** Spatial-hash weld with configurable epsilon, index remap. Deterministic ordering — this is the classic determinism leak, so T-004 must cover it explicitly. | M | A-001, T-004 |
| | | ***Nothing inside one volume needed welding, and that is the finding that shaped the module.*** Marching Cubes keys its vertices on the grid edge and the dual methods key theirs on the cell, so sharing is by construction and every reference mesh already reports `duplicate_vertices == 0`. Dong et al. (2018, `10.1109/icra.2018.8463157`) arrive at the same design from the other direction — their contribution is distributing vertices on discrete edges so duplicates cannot be allocated at all. **The duplicates are entirely at chunk seams**, which is why this ticket could not have been done before G-001. |
| | | ***The rule is stated because it is a choice, not a fact: epsilon-closeness is not transitive.*** "Weld everything within ε" does not define equivalence classes and cannot be implemented as written. What is well defined is first fit against the vertices already **kept**: walk in input index order, join the lowest-indexed kept vertex within ε, or become one. Two consequences are asserted rather than left to be discovered — a chain `a ~ b ~ c` whose ends are more than ε apart yields **two** representatives, so no vertex is ever moved further than ε from where the extractor put it; and the tie-break is the lowest index rather than the nearest or the first the 27-cell probe reaches, so the answer does not depend on the order those cells are visited in. |
| | | ***The seam fixture is `h = 4/35`, taken from M-32's search rather than picked.*** M-32 measured that a chunk seam is bit-exact only at a power-of-two cell size, and that 22% of random `(origin, h, cells, chunk)` combinations disagree by an ulp. A weld keyed on equality passes at `h = 0.125` and silently leaves the seam open at `4/35` — correct on the fixture, wrong in the field — so the test runs at the spacing that can tell them apart, and a second test runs at `0.125` to show one epsilon covers both. **Measured:** two chunks of a unit sphere, `273 → 259` vertices, boundary edges `85 → 59`, `duplicate_vertices 14 → 0`, `χ 2 → 1` — two discs glued along an arc. |
| | | ***`duplicate_vertices` is an upper bound on what a weld removes, not a prediction of it (M-47).*** The validator asks whether *any* earlier vertex is within ε; the welder asks for the lowest-indexed *kept* one, and they part company exactly where a chain exists. **Predicted before running and both halves held:** equal on a real seam, where duplicates are pairs an ulp apart with no chains (14 and 14), and different on a constructed chain (2 against 1). T-008's doc claim that the count is "how many a first-fit welder could remove" is now precise about the direction. |
| | | *One lattice, not two.* `weld::Lattice` carries M-18's anchor-at-the-mesh-minimum rule and `validate`'s duplicate scan now calls it instead of holding a copy. If the two disagreed about which cells are probed, the reported count would describe a different neighbourhood than the weld it reports on. |
| | | *`MeshBuffer::append` came with it*, because a seam is not weldable until both chunks' vertices are in one buffer and the index shift is the step a caller gets wrong. It concatenates and welds nothing. |
| | | *`Error::InvalidWeldEpsilon` rejects zero* rather than reading it as "weld exact matches only" — which is precisely the case M-32 says fails at a seam. T-004 covers the weld directly: three runs including one into a reused buffer, bit-compared through `total_cmp`, plus a test that a warmed-up `Welder`'s scratch gives the same answer as a fresh one. |
