| | | *The layout is anchored so the seam falls at `x = 0`, through the middle of the torus.* The first version anchored at `[-2, -2, -2]` and the two chunks clipped a sliver off one corner of the field — the machinery was visible and the point was not. The chunk floor of 24 cells is what keeps a chunk wide enough to contain the torus at the coarser spacing. |
| | | *`ISOMESH_SAMPLES` and `ISOMESH_WELD` were added to the shared harness for this.* The harness's own documentation says a capture needs no keyboard, and that was **not true of resolution**: the committed `e111` screenshots are of a 19³ grid with no way to ask for one, so they could not be regenerated from a command line. Adding it also verified them — re-capturing at `ISOMESH_SAMPLES=19` reproduces the 39 non-manifold edges and 61 vertices the README caption claims. |
| ☑ | **E-202** | `game_dig` — first-person, click to carve tunnels | The core Minecraft/Deep Rock loop. Re-mesh is imperceptible. Chunks-touched count on screen. | G-002, G-003 |
| | | ***The acceptance numbers, live and on screen.*** A typical carve: **3 chunks re-meshed in 0.41 ms**, **265 of 1,728 cells in the brush's bounding box = E1 15.3%**, 3 of 8 chunks in the box dirty. E1 lands inside M-33's offline 15–36%, and the 756 cells whose *sample* moved against the 265 that actually re-mesh is a ratio of **2.85×**, inside M-34's 2.8–3.7×. **This is the first time either number has been measured on the interactive path they were written to justify** (M-50). |
| | | *The chunk outlines are the ticket's "chunks-touched count on screen", made spatial.* The count says how much an edit cost; the boxes say where, and only the boxes show that a brush straddling a corner costs eight chunks rather than one. |
| | | ***`ISOMESH_AUTOCARVE` exists because a screenshot cannot click.*** The acceptance criterion is about what happens while someone is holding the mouse down, so without a scripted path the example could have been committed compiling, rendering, and silently not carving at all. It drives the same code path a click does, prints one line per edit, and the committed screenshot is of a tunnel that was actually dug. |
| | | ***A claim in the module docs was wrong and the measurement corrected it.*** "Every field sample walks the whole log, so ms/edit grows with it" is true about the mechanism and implies proportionality that does not hold. Measured over 60 carves, median ms per re-meshed chunk: **0.158 / 0.354 / 0.525 / 0.589** for logs of 1–15 / 16–30 / 31–45 / 46–60 — **3.7× for 7× the log, and flattening.** The docs now carry the table and stop short of claiming why, which has not been measured. |
| | | *Two API additions, each with a first consumer rather than a guess.* `ChunkLayout::cell_of` converts a world point to a global cell index, which is what `mark_edit` takes and what every brush-driven edit needs; writing `floor((p − origin)/h)` by hand at each call site is the arithmetic M-32 is about. Its test caught immediately that it does **not** round-trip through a cell corner at a non-power-of-two spacing (M-49). And the spacing here is `0.125` deliberately: each chunk is its own `Mesh3d` with no weld, so it uses the power-of-two case where M-32 says the seam is already exact. |
| ☑ | **A-003** | **Marching Tetrahedra.** 6-tet decomposition of the cube (document which one — the choice affects the output). Unambiguous by construction, so this is the topological reference the others get compared against. Expect a large triangle count; record it. | M | A-001 |
| | | ***Derived, because there was nothing to transcribe.*** Doi & Koide 1991 has no DOI, is not in the local corpus and could not be obtained — the same situation as A-001 and the same answer. The decomposition is **Kuhn's / Freudenthal's**: the six monotone paths from corner 0 to corner 7, one per axis ordering, every one containing the main diagonal. Winding is computed rather than tabulated, by cross product against the direction away from an inside corner in doubled integer coordinates, so it is exact at compile time; a separate test recomputes it from the geometry independently. |
| | | ***The five-tetrahedron tiling is rejected for a reason, not a preference.*** It is cheaper and **chiral** — neighbours must alternate handedness or their shared face is split by two different diagonals, which is a crack. Kuhn's needs no alternation: this cell splits its `+x` face on `1–7` and the next splits its `−x` face on `0–6`, and those are the same two world points. Asserted on all three axes. |
| | | ***Both published figures are wrong for this implementation (M-51).*** "2–3× more vertices" measures **2.86–3.91×** and covers only the two roughest fields; `box_exact` is 3.91×. Lewiner et al.'s "weaker geometrical accuracy" measures **4.3%** — symmetric Hausdorff `1.4386e-3` against Marching Cubes' `1.3798e-3` on a unit sphere at 64³. So the trade is **~3× the triangles for ~4% worse geometry**, sharper than either source gives. The Marching Cubes figure reproduces M-10's `1.380e-3` exactly. |
| | | ***P-1 was confirmed where its assumption holds and exceeded where it does not, and two attempts to explain the gap both failed (M-52, O-15).*** Orientation does not move the ratio — four plane orientations agree within 1%. Curvature does not either — a sphere swept 0.3 to 1.8 converges *down* onto P-1's 2.99 as it flattens, where flatness ought to push it toward the plane's 3.94. A plane and a large sphere are the same shape at cell scale and differ by 30%. Recorded as an open question rather than a third guess. |
| | | *It takes the strict `SurfaceGate::Closed` in the property suite, and here that is structural rather than observed:* a tetrahedron's four values determine its linear interpolant, so there is no ambiguity to resolve and no fan to choose. That is what makes it the topological reference the others are compared against — worth having after a session that found two topology defects in Marching Cubes by reasoning about one algorithm against itself. |
| | | *Golden fixture 84 → 105 rows. The resolution sweep gets an `Extractor` impl and is deliberately **not** wired into its `main()`:* adding a row would rewrite the committed CSV that ✗14, M-19, M-20, M-21, M-22 and O-11 all quote exact figures from. That re-measurement belongs to **M-001**, which A-003 now unblocks. |
| ☑ | **M-001a** | **`bench_shootout` — the four that already exist.** Marching Cubes / Marching Cubes 33 / Surface Nets / Dual Contouring, identical fields and grids, one process, one run. Table: ms, verts, tris, non-manifold edges, self-int/1k, Hausdorff. CSV to `docs/measurements/`. **Split out 2026-08-12: this is unblocked today.** The comparison does not exist in the literature for post-2020 hardware, and Surface Nets has no credible published timings at all (V-17) — there is no reason for the publishable result to wait on a fifth algorithm that is a baseline rather than a contender. | M | A-007, T-006 |
| ☑ | **M-001b** | **Add the Marching Tetrahedra row** to the shootout, and settle O-13 / O-14 against their pre-registered values (3.0× vertices; ≈2.6e-3 Hausdorff at 64³). | S | M-001a, A-003 |
| | | *Done together, because the split's premise expired.* M-001a was carved out on the grounds that "there is no reason for the publishable result to wait on a fifth algorithm" — A-003 landed the same day, so the bench was written with the Marching Tetrahedra row from the start and both tickets close on one commit. |
| | | ***The headline is that Marching Cubes wins the property nobody credits it with (M-53).*** Seven fields, two grids, one process: it and the decider are the only entries with **both** zero non-manifold edges and zero self-intersections. Marching Tetrahedra is manifold and self-intersects (3.405 per 1k on `csg_difference`); Surface Nets is intersection-free and non-manifold (128 edges); Dual Contouring is neither. Three of the four corners of that 2×2 are occupied, and the crude baseline holds the good one. |
| | | ***Dual Contouring wins the property it was built for by two orders of magnitude (M-54).*** Symmetric Hausdorff at 65³ against Marching Cubes: `box_exact` **101×**, `thin_plate` **77.9×**, `csg_difference` 3.7× — and `sphere` 1.2×, `torus` 1.6×. Two orders of magnitude exactly where the features are sharp and nothing at all where they are not. |
| | | ***O-13 confirmed exactly, O-14 falsified (M-55).*** The vertex ratio is `3.036 / 3.026 / 3.003` at 33/49/65³, converging from above onto the predicted 3.0. The Hausdorff prediction of `2.6e-3` and `1.86×` measures **1.4386e-3** and **1.043×** — and Marching Tetrahedra beats Surface Nets (2.251e-3) rather than losing to it. The clause flagged as counterintuitive, "more vertices **and** worse accuracy", is the half that does not hold. |
| | | *Three cross-checks that this table measures what earlier tickets measured.* Dual Contouring's `13.837` and `3.118` per 1k reproduce **M-28**'s clamped `fbm_terrain` and `gyroid` exactly; Surface Nets' triangle ratio of `0.977–1.001` is **✗1**'s `F_sn = F_mc + 2χ` from the other side; Marching Tetrahedra's `2.902–3.937` spread is **M-52**'s octant rule. |
| | | *Two columns are conditional on the fields' own metadata, not the bench's opinion.* Hausdorff only where `is_exact_distance()` — the quantity T-003 computes against `gyroid` is not a distance and printing it would invent a number. Self-intersection at 33³ only: an all-pairs test behind a broadphase, against meshes carrying up to ~126,000 triangles. |
| | | *The timing column is deliberately the weaker one and says so in the module docs.* Three timed runs across 70 points, so it is a few percent noisy — the decider reads 0.89× on `sphere` where it must be 1.00×. `resolution_sweep` stays the timing authority and this is the topology authority. What the column does support is the shape: the decider is free (M-42), and **Surface Nets is slower than Marching Cubes on every field**, which is ✗14 arriving a third time. |
| ☑ | **A-005** | **Greedy quads / blocky.** Binary occupancy, per-face masks, greedy merge. The budget end of the tradeoff table and the comparison baseline for triangle counts. | M | T-001 |
| | | ***The published `2.76×` is one scene's number, and the range is `1.70×` to `256×` (M-56).*** Same occupancy, merge on against merge off: `gyroid` 1.70×, `sphere` 1.94×, `torus` 2.69×, `fbm_terrain` 4.60×, `csg_difference` 10.64×, `box_exact` **256×** — six quads at every resolution, 12 triangles at 17³, 33³ and 65³ alike. Predicted before running that it would not reproduce as a constant and that a box would collapse while a sphere would not. Third source in this repo whose single figure turns out to be field-dependent, after ✗14 and M-51. |
| | | ***Greedy merging manufactures T-junctions and no weld removes them (M-57).*** Split vertices are deliberate — a cube corner has three normals — so the raw buffer is open: five edges per merged quad, four unshared. A-013's weld closes it wherever quads meet corner to corner and **cannot** close a T-junction, because the vertex where several short quads meet does not exist on the long quad's edge. Sphere at 33³: boundary `2568 → 768`, 70% closed. `box_exact`: `24 → 0`, closed, `χ = 2` — the control that makes this a mechanism rather than an observation. |
| | | *So the T-001 gate is met conditionally and the condition is stated rather than waived:* welded, and on a field whose merge leaves no T-junctions. That is the honest reading of "passes the validity suite" for an algorithm whose output is deliberately not a manifold index buffer, and it is why the tests weld before validating instead of asserting on the raw output. |
| | | *One sample per cell centre, not eight corners.* A blocky mesher's premise is that a cell is one thing; asking eight corners and reducing them to one bit would be doing Marching Cubes' work and discarding the answer. The consequence is measured rather than assumed: `thin_plate` is 0.4 cells thick and comes back with **zero triangles**, because no cell centre is inside it. A feature thinner than a cell does not exist to this algorithm. |
| | | *The domain boundary is capped* — a cell outside the grid counts as empty, so a solid cell at the edge emits a face. That differs from every other extractor here, which lets the surface leave through the sides, and it is correct for this one: a blocky mesh of a half-filled box is a closed box. `fbm_terrain` therefore comes back closed here and open under Marching Cubes, which is a difference in what the algorithms are. |
| | | *Normals cost nothing:* `±1` on one axis, exactly, with no gradient evaluation anywhere, where every other extractor calls `Sdf::gradient` once per vertex. Golden fixture 105 → 126 rows. Not added to M-001's shootout: it meshes a **different surface**, so its Hausdorff against the true isosurface is not comparable with the others' and a row there would invite exactly the wrong comparison. |
| ☑ | **A-010** | **Manifold Dual Contouring.** Vertex splitting so each cell can emit >1 vertex where topology demands it. **Acceptance:** `non_manifold_edges == 0` on `gyroid` and `csg_difference`, where plain Dual Contouring will not manage it. | L | A-007 |
| | | ***The paper the research docs list as "genuinely absent, blocking" is in the corpus, and it says the manifold guarantee is not its own.*** `10.1109/TVCG.2007.1012` is indexed locally (stem `dualsimp_tvcg`), against three research docs recording it as an acquisition gap. Read this session: §2.2 and §3 say the uniform-grid construction is **Nielson's Dual MC** (IEEE Vis 2004, `10.1109/VISUAL.2004.28`) — *"one vertex with each cycle of a modified MC table … each edge is associated with exactly one vertex"* — plus DC's QEF per cycle. The 2007 contribution is the **octree vertex-clustering criterion**, which needs an octree this crate has not got and G-004 does not want. So A-010 as implemented is Hermite Dual MC, and the ticket title names the wrong paper. |
| | | *Beware two different 2004 papers called "Dual Marching Cubes":* Nielson's `10.1109/visual.2004.28` (dual of the MC **patch** surface, one vertex per cycle) and Schaefer & Warren's `10.1109/pccga.2004.1348336` (MC on a **dual grid**). Only the first is this algorithm. |
| | | *The cycles are **derived, not transcribed**, and that is the whole guarantee.* Nielson is paywalled, exactly as Doi & Koide was at A-003, and rule 5 forbids inventing the table. Nothing was invented: `segment_links(case, joined)` — the function the 256-case Marching Cubes table is *built* from — already links cut edges into the cycles the paper means. Walking it makes the output the dual of **this crate's** Marching Cubes, the one measured at zero non-manifold edges and zero self-intersections in M-53, rather than the dual of some other paper's. |
| | | ***Acceptance met, and half of it was vacuous as written (M-58).*** `gyroid` 33³ **15 non-manifold edges and 40 non-manifold vertices → 0 and 0**; 49³ **48 and 99 → 0 and 0**. But `csg_difference` measures **0 under plain Dual Contouring already** at both resolutions, so it was never a field DC "will not manage". The test therefore asserts the comparison is non-vacuous rather than only asserting the new number. |
| | | ***A second, unrelated non-manifold mechanism turned up, and the arithmetic identifies it (M-59).*** The dual of a manifold surface is a manifold **complex**; an index buffer cannot carry two distinct edges between one pair of vertices, so where two cells share a face with two segments and each puts both in one cycle, the dual edges collapse into one edge with four faces. Found by the strict property gate, which shrank to the **✗15 fixture** — the same three-sphere union at `h = 2/3`. A collapse costs exactly one edge, so `χ_dual − χ_mc == non_manifold_edges` must hold: measured `1 − 0 == 1`, and `0 == 0` at every resolution from 9³ up. Pinned in both directions; O-16 owns the question of removing it. |
| | | ***The cost is zero on five of seven fields (M-60).*** Extra vertices over plain Dual Contouring: `gyroid` 3.13% / 2.05% / 0.53% and `fbm_terrain` 1.70% / 0.84% / 0.77% at 17³/25³/33³, everything else **exactly 0 at every resolution**. The rate *falls* with refinement, which is M-15 from the other side, and it is the first curve for **O-10**. Nielson's *"about 1.3% of all configurations"* counts the case table, not a scene. |
| | | ***P-6 falsified: self-intersection gets worse, not better (M-61).*** `gyroid` **3.118 → 5.669** per 1k, `fbm_terrain` **13.837 → 15.434**, five fields 0 → 0. The prediction reasoned from M-29's *"the residue is exactly A-010's problem"*; M-29's attribution holds — only the splitting fields have a non-zero count — and the inference did not. Two vertices in one cell is exactly what breaks the within-cell partition Manson & Schaefer's clamp guarantee rests on. ✗2's ODC figure was on the record and should have outweighed M-29 before registering. |
| | | *The shared dual engine was widened rather than copied.* `VertexRule` now reports a set of **surface components**, each owning some of the cell's twelve edges, and the quad walk looks up `(cell, edge) → vertex` instead of `cell → vertex`. Surface Nets and Dual Contouring answer "the only one" for every edge, and the golden fixture proves the refactor is bit-neutral: **21 rows added, zero existing hashes changed**, and `topology_is_identical_to_surface_nets` still passes. Fixture 126 → 147 rows. |
| | | *A sphere's output is byte-identical to plain Dual Contouring's,* asserted directly on positions and indices at 17³ and 33³ — the strongest available statement that the per-cycle solve degenerates exactly to the whole-cell solve when the cycle is the whole cell. |
| | | ***Amended at A-017 (2026-08-15): the headline property was conditional and nobody knew on what.*** *"`manifold_dual_contouring` is the entry that takes the zero"* held on seven reference fields because **not one of them can produce the configuration** — 0 of 68,385 surface cells have an interior ambiguity (M-208). On A-002e's `noise_cavity` it returns **30 non-manifold edges at 17³ and 64 at 33³**, which falsified P-5, pre-registered before this ticket ran. **The mechanism is exact and is a limit of the published algorithm** (M-225): an ambiguous face has all four edges cut, and one vertex per cycle per cell means that when all four land in one cycle on each side, all four dual quads connect the same pair of cell vertices — four quads on one dual edge. Schaefer, Ju and Warren separate sheets *within* a cell and never claimed two crossed edges of one **shared** face resolving to the same cycle pair. **A-017 chose to document rather than fix**, because splitting the cycle needs a second vertex where the paper gives one and would no longer be their algorithm. Counts stay pinned as whole censuses so they fail if they spread *and* if they vanish. |
| ☑ | **A-011a** | **The Transvoxel transition cell case table.** Derived, not transcribed, and checked against the dissertation's own equivalence-class count. | M | A-001 |
| | | ***The dissertation listed as "genuinely absent, blocking" is in the corpus — the second one today.*** `transvoxel_dissertation_lengyel2010` is indexed locally, against `catalog-v2.md:629` recording Transvoxel as an acquisition gap. Same as A-010's `10.1109/TVCG.2007.1012`. Two for two, so the acquisition lists in `docs/research/` are stale rather than the corpus being thin; **search home-still before believing a doc that says a paper is missing**. Recorded as M-63. |
| | | ***The derivation reproduces Lengyel's 73 equivalence classes exactly, from geometry defined without reference to his numbering.*** §4.3: *"Observing the orbits of each of the 512 cases under the action of the group `D₈` or `D₈ × ℤ₂`, as appropriate, yields exactly 73 distinct equivalence classes."* Measured: **73**, and the trivial class holds both all-inside and all-outside as he describes. Second, independent handle: Table 4.8's split into **51** classes reaching four-or-fewer inside and **22** inverse classes — also reproduced. A derivation that hit 73 by luck would be unlikely to split 51/22 as well. |
| | | *The nine-face reading is what made it the problem A-001 had already solved.* The left cell's boundary is a closed oriented surface of four quadrants, one half-resolution face and four **laterals that reduce to triangles** — each lateral's two depth edges join a sample to its own duplicate and can never be cut. A three-cornered face carries 0 or 2 crossings and has no pairing to choose, so the transition itself needs no ambiguity rule, which is exactly why the dissertation names only *"an ambiguous half-resolution face"* and *"ambiguous quadrants"*. Asserted, not assumed. |
| | | ***A lateral link does not always cross the resolution boundary, and the exception is the point (M-64).*** The test was written as "a lateral always joins a fine edge to a coarse one" and failed on the case with only the midpoint sample inside: both fine sub-edges cut, coarse edge not, so the lateral links **fine to fine** and caps the bump off entirely on the fine side. That is correct — the coarse neighbour cannot represent that feature and must not be asked to. Over all configurations: **2,080 links stitch the seam, 1,128 cap a sub-coarse feature.** |
| | | *Two numbers A-011b will need:* the longest cycle is **12** edges and a cell produces at most **4** of them — the same slot budget the cube needed at A-010, so the `CellVertices` machinery transfers unchanged. |
| | | *No world-space geometry here, deliberately.* This ticket is the table and its proofs; placement, winding and the seam assertion are A-011b. Splitting an `L` was the backlog's own advice and the split fell on a clean seam: everything above is checkable without a grid. |
| ☑ | **A-012** | **Normal estimation strategies.** Analytic gradient / central differences / area-weighted face normals, selectable. **Acceptance:** all three produce unit-length normals; analytic and central-difference agree within tolerance on `sphere`. | S | A-001 |
| | | *A **post-pass**, not a setting on six extractors.* `normals::recompute(&mut MeshBuffer, &sdf, strategy)` re-derives every normal in a finished buffer, which works for all six algorithms and for anything downstream of them — a weld, a merge, a collider bake. Six `set_normal_strategy` setters would have been six copies of one branch and six places for the default to drift, and none of them would serve a caller holding a mesh that has already been welded. |
| | | *The no-op is assertable, which is the point of doing it this way.* `AnalyticGradient` is exactly what every extractor does inline, so recomputing with it must return **bit-identical** normals — asserted. If the post-pass and the extractors ever diverge, that test says so. |
| | | ***Acceptance met, and the second half converges at `h²` (M-65).*** Analytic against central differences at `step = h` on `sphere`, worst and mean angle: **0.460° / 0.299°** at 17³, **0.121° / 0.079°** at 33³, **0.031° / 0.020°** at 65³. Ratios `3.76` and `3.92` — `h²`, asserted as a range rather than only printed. Same convergence order M-12 measured for *position* error, now for direction. |
| | | ***Area-weighted normals disagree with the field by an amount that does not fall with resolution on a sharp field (M-66).*** Mean angle from the analytic gradient: `sphere` **3.25° → 2.16° → 1.08°** at 17/33/65³ and `torus` **11.65° → 6.07° → 2.45°**, both falling; `box_exact` mean falls **13.55° → 6.73° → 3.34°** but its **worst is 35.796° at all three resolutions, identical to six figures**. Refining a grid does not soften a corner. Asserted as the mechanism — the box's worst is resolution-invariant, the sphere's worst falls by at least a quarter — rather than as a pinned constant. |
| | | *`Error::DegenerateNormal` rather than a substituted `[0, 0, 1]`.* A zero gradient, or an area-weighted vertex with no incident area, is reported. Writing a plausible default there gives a mesh that renders and is wrong in a way nothing downstream can attribute, which is the one-path rule's whole subject. A collinear triangle is the test. |
| | | *Area weighting comes free from **not** normalising the face normal:* `(b−a)×(c−a)` has magnitude twice the area, so summing raw cross products *is* the area-weighted sum, and dividing each by its own length is the step that would throw the weighting away. |
| ☑ | **A-014a** | **Subgrid MT — normal coordinates.** The encoding, its two validity conditions, and the decomposition into normal triangles and quads. | M | A-003 |
| ☑ | **A-014b** | **Subgrid MT — the surface fill.** §3.2's primal reconstruction: triangulate the boundary curves §3.1 produces so the output is intersection-free, via the four cases (corner cuts, parallel quads, octagons, single closed loop) and the Steiner-point rules. **Acceptance:** a configuration that `decompose` rejects still meshes, and the result is conforming across a two-tet fixture. **§3.1 is done** — `subgrid::curves` reconstructs and classifies the boundary curves, and M-79 pins the conformity property it rests on. **Theorems B.3 and B.6 are read, and the first two cases are in** `subgrid::surface`: ordered cycles, corner cuts, quads, and the `(d₁, d₂)` pattern with Theorem B.4's component count and Corollary B.6's length checked as equalities against §3.1's own reconstruction (M-82). Classic Marching Tetrahedra needs nothing further (M-81). **Octagons are in too** — `ℓ = 8` forces `d₁ = d₂ = m`, so the case is a property of the pattern rather than a separate test, and the `m` Steiner points go along the segment joining the midpoints of the `2m`-crossing pair, innermost loop nearest `e`. **The single-loop case is in** — one Steiner point at the centre of mass, which is in the convex hull the paper allows it anywhere within. M-83 pins how much the intersection-free assertion is worth, and it is not uniform: cross-loop folds are visible, intra-fan ones are not, so the zero is real for quads and octagons and **entirely vacuous for single loops** (190 of 190 pairs skipped on `(3, 2)`), which a test now asserts rather than lets pass as coverage. **Subdivision's combinatorial half is in** — `Subdivision::label` finds the `i, j, k, l` Property II guarantees (the stencil is asymmetric, so the labelling decides everything), and M-84 verifies that all four sub-tets it produces are themselves normal configurations, tightly: three faces per sub-tet meet the triangle inequality with equality. **§3.2.2's classification and its diagonal type are in** — the parity rule (`b_ij = e_ij mod 2`, `p = b₀₁ + b₀₂ + b₀₃`: contractible at 0, diagonal at 2, corner at 1 or 3), with all three types shown reachable over a 4,096-configuration sweep, and the diagonal spanning disk as a centroid fan. M-85 records the structural correction that made it run at all. **The acceptance criterion's conformity half is met and its meaning is now precise** (M-86): two tets filled independently carry no unaccounted boundary edge in their shared face, where "unaccounted" is the right gate rather than "none" — a segment one tet discards as an open curve legitimately becomes mesh boundary, and the first version of that test asserted the stronger property and failed. **Coverage is pinned at 3,394 of 4,096** configurations meshing completely over counts 0–3 per edge, with `NoPattern` and `Inconsistent` both zero (M-87) — so Property II held everywhere, and **the entire remaining gap is one construction, not four**. **What remains:** §3.2.2's **corner and contractible** types, whose disks live in the tet *boundary* rather than its interior. **The vertex-labelling rule was retrieved 2026-08-12 and is no longer a gap** — it is what the segment labelling below is defined against: *"When `γ` is of corner type, we mark the distinguished vertex as inside and all other vertices as outside. When `γ` is contractible, all vertices are 'outside', and when `γ` is diagonal we do not require an inside/outside distinction."* **Both labelling rules are implemented and cross-checked** (`Side`, `corner_sides`, `distinguished_corner`, `edge_sides`): the distinguished vertex is the one whose three incident edges all have odd parity, and M-88 pins the property that couples the two rules — walking an edge from its lower corner and flipping at each crossing arrives at exactly the side the far corner was independently assigned, over all 4,096 configurations. **What is left is the planar arrangement**: split each face `σ` along `γ`'s segments into polygons `σ \ γ`, then emit any polygon bound by "inside" segments and segments of `γ`; if one of its vertices coincides with the inside vertex of a corner loop, omit that vertex; and for corner-type loops also emit a triangle at the corner. **The planar arrangement and the contractible disk are both in.** `face_regions` splits a face into `σ \ γ` by peeling innermost chords, checked over 4,096 configurations by edge-piece conservation, `regions == chords/2 + 1`, and the order-sensitive "a crossing bounds exactly two regions, a corner one" (M-90 records the scoop-ownership bug that last one caught). `fill_boundary_disk` emits the qualifying regions for all three non-normal types, and **§3.2.2 is complete: 4,096 of 4,096 configurations mesh**, with `NoPattern` and `Inconsistent` both zero (M-92). The disk introduces no new vertices, so it is cheaper than any Steiner case (M-91), and 105 configurations correctly triangulate to nothing because their inside region is a bigon — zero area until A-014d insets it. **What remains is subdivision's geometric half only**: placing crossings on the four spokes, building each sub-tet's `TetCrossings` with the right edge-orientation mapping, and recursing, bounded by Theorem C.1. The 4,096 sweep never reaches it — `ℓ > 8` needs larger `d₁, d₂` than three crossings per edge supply — so it needs purpose-built fixtures rather than a coverage number. The disk lives in the tet *boundary*, so its triangles are coplanar with the faces by construction, and a scoop realises as a chord lying *along* its own edge, giving the region it bounds zero area. Both are the specified output, not defects — A-014d is what insets them. A geometric build collapses every scoop before A-014d can. **`thin_plate` needs only the contractible half** (M-89): 192 contractible loops, zero corner, so the distinguished vertex, the corner triangle and the vertex-omission rule are all corner-type-only and can land second. Also still open: subdivision's geometric half — placing crossings on the four spokes and recursing per sub-tet, bounded by Theorem C.1 — which the 4,096 sweep does not reach at all, so it needs its own fixtures. `Unfilled` names which of these a configuration reached, so nothing emits a plausible wrong answer meanwhile. **§3.2.3 (simplicial embedding) is *not* in this ticket** — see A-014d. | L | A-014a |

Against that, **the case that looked hardest turned out cheapest**: the corner/contractible spanning disk needed the only planar arrangement in §3.2, and introduces no vertices at all because it lives in the tet boundary (M-91). |

And subdivision's 30 self-intersections were vertex duplication, not folds (M-93). |
| | | ***Two zeros needed proving they could be non-zero.*** T-002 skips triangle pairs sharing a vertex, and every case here is a fan or a boundary disk — so a single loop's zero is **vacuous**, 190 of 190 pairs skipped (M-83), and a mutation must permute apexes rather than merge them. **§3.2.3 is deliberately not here**: the disks are *supposed* to come out degenerate, and 105 configurations correctly triangulate to nothing (V-21, M-92). A-014d owns the inset. |
| | | ***Classic Marching Tetrahedra is provably the 0/1 case of this encoding, checked against A-003's own table.*** The paper says marching tetrahedra *"reinvented a small piece of this story"*; taking `eᵢⱼ = 1` exactly where a tet's corner signs differ, decomposing, and counting a quad as the two triangles it becomes reproduces A-003's triangle count on **all 96 (tet, configuration) pairs** — **48 corner cuts and 36 diagonal cuts** over six tets, which is P-4's 8-and-6 arithmetic arrived at from the opposite direction. That test is what connects the new machinery to machinery already trusted, and it would fail on a wrong complementary pairing, a wrong incidence vector or a wrong corner-coordinate formula. |
| | | ***95.6% of normal configurations are invisible to a sign test (M-67).*** Over every edge-coordinate vector with counts 0–3, **181** are normal curve systems and only **8** are classic; the other **173** carry two or more crossings on some edge, where a sign test reads only the parity and sees the classic one with the same parities. That is the size of the gap A-014 exists to close, and it is measured rather than argued. |
| | | *Both of the paper's own counterexamples are pinned.* `e = (2,1,1,2,1,1)` has a perfectly normal *boundary* and no normal-surface decomposition, because the two quads it needs intersect — §2.3's *"only one of the coordinates `qᵢⱼ` can be nonzero"*. `e = (1,3,0,3,3,0)` is not even a normal curve system. So `decompose` returning `None` is a routine signal, not an error, and it is precisely the signal A-014b's general reconstruction responds to. |
| | | *The paper's edge ordering was **not** adopted.* It writes `e := (e₀₁, e₀₂, e₀₃, e₂₃, e₁₃, e₁₂)` so complementary pairs sit three apart; this crate keeps A-003's lexicographic `TET_EDGES` and recovers the pairing as `5 - edge`. That is load-bearing rather than cosmetic — a quad is *defined* by which opposite pair it separates — so `complementary_edges_share_no_corner` asserts the two conventions agree instead of trusting them to. |
| | | *`decompose` verifies by reconstruction rather than trusting its own algebra:* a candidate is accepted only when the forward map `e = M·n` reproduces the input exactly. 2,560 generated normal surfaces round-trip. |

The camera's own chunk is at distance zero and therefore resident at any radius, which is what stops a player falling through the world they are standing on. |

| ☑ | **E-116** | `marching_cubes_interior` — **the MC33 interior decider made visible, and the 12.6% the classic test gets wrong.** The sibling of E-102: that one resolves an ambiguous *face*, this one an ambiguous *cell*. | S | A-002c |

Drawn as points and not a polyline **on purpose**: joining the last sample before the pole to the first after it would draw a chord across the discontinuity and hide the one thing the example exists for. |
| | | ***The HUD prints both verdicts every frame.*** Chernyaev's numerator-only test against the corrected one. | ☑ | **A-014g** | **Subgrid MT — a cross-cell shared vertex table, so a vertex has an identity and not just a location.** Split out of A-014d as the half that needs no unread figure. | | | ***The key is a property of the grid, and the reason is one line of arithmetic.*** A crossing is named by its tet edge's two grid points and the root's ordinal. `TETS[t]` orders corners by inclusion; offsets are the grid point minus the cell origin; subtracting the same origin from both preserves the componentwise comparison — so inclusion is equivalent to `P ≤ Q` componentwise, which names the same direction from whichever cell the edge is viewed. `FacePoint` already counted `index` from the lower corner for precisely this reason, so the design was anticipated by A-014b's own doc comment. |
| | | ***Measured: 5.01× fewer vertices, zero triangles changed (M-168).*** Across the 21 golden rows 389,146 → 77,605, per row 4.76× to 5.08×, and **0 of all 168 golden rows changed a triangle count** — the strongest available check that the sharing did not also move geometry. |
| | | ***And a completeness limit that is a finding rather than a shortfall (M-169).*** Sharing is complete **exactly when no root lands on a grid sample point**: `torus` and `fbm_terrain` have zero on a lattice point and the weld removes zero; `box_exact` has all 1262 on one and loses 924. A root at an edge *endpoint* sits where up to 24 tet edges meet, so it has one position and a different `(edge, index)` on each. That is how the field sits on the grid, not the algorithm — M-94's fixture trap, structurally. The remedy stays an identity (name such a crossing by its grid point) and is left to its own commit rather than folded in. |
| | | ***Steiner points are deliberately not shared.*** A centroid is a property of one tetrahedron; giving it a key would assert an identity it does not have. `TetPatch::crossings` makes the "crossings first, then Steiner points" ordering machine-readable instead of only documented, and `crossing_order` is one function used by both the recorder and the indexer so the two cannot drift. |
| | | *Subdivision, octagons and the single-loop case were measured first and **fire on no reference field at any of the seven** — only corner cuts, non-normal loops and quads occur. That is what made the keying tractable, and it also says the validity suite exercises three of §3.2's five cases not at all, though each has its own unit test in `surface/tests.rs`.* |
| ☑ | **A-002c** | **The corrected MC33 interior decider — Custodio's sign, not Chernyaev's `F(t)`.** Split out of A-002b because it is the half that needs no architecture: a pure function of eight numbers, with no vertex cache, no table and no triangulation. | M | A-002 |
| | | ***The mechanism, verified and re-derived (V-24).*** The swept saddle is `f(x_c(t)) = F(t)/Δ(t)` with `F` quadratic and `Δ` **linear**, and Chernyaev tracks `F` alone. A quotient of a quadratic by a linear is a hyperbola, so Δ's root is a **pole** and the sign jumps across it — three sign changes where a quadratic allows two. The three coefficients were expanded from `A_t = A₀ + tα` **before** being compared with the paper's, and agree term for term; that is how rule 5 is met without transcribing a formula. |
| | | ***Evaluated exactly, with no tolerance and no iteration.*** `F/Δ` has constant sign between its own roots and its pole, so the breakpoints are enumerated and one interior point of each subinterval is tested. The answer is a finite sign computation rather than a sampled sweep. |
| | | ***The acceptance is a counterexample this repo derived rather than copied (✗22).*** Appendix A's numbers survived the PDF conversion with their letters scrambled, and no grouping reproduces the paper's own `c = 0.1701`; one grouping lands within 0.0002 if you read a digit the text does not say, which is exactly the trap rule 5 exists for. So the fixture is built instead: `lo = [0.1, −2, 10, −2]`, `hi = [−10, 2, −0.1, 2]`, both faces ambiguous, `F` convex and negative at both ends hence negative throughout, Δ running +14.1 → −14.1. **Chernyaev's numerator test says separated; the corrected test says joined.** It has the same sign structure as Appendix A's, which was not aimed at and is therefore evidence. |
| | | ***How much it matters, measured: 12.6% (M-165).*** Of 15,625 opposed face pairs — the only structure that can put a pole inside the sweep — all 15,625 have one and **1,966 are decided differently**. Not to be confused with Custodio's once-in-10,000-fields, which is small because the family is rare. |
| | | ***And one thing found by trying to assert too much (M-166).*** A test claiming the saddle is bit-identical under a diagonal-preserving rotation could not be justified: `ambiguity` gets that from multiplication being commutative, and this module's denominator is a subtraction chain a rotation permutes. The claim was weakened to a tolerance and the gap written down as A-002b's to close — two cells could otherwise disagree about a tunnel across a shared face. |
| | | *`Error::DegenerateSweep` rather than a default: a zero bilinear denominator means the face has no saddle, so the criterion has no answer there. It cannot happen on an ambiguous face, so reaching it means the caller asked the wrong question — and a plausible topology would hide that.* |
| ☑ | **GPU-010b** | **An indirect mesh draw, so the last four bytes stop coming home.** | L | GPU-010a |
| | | ***The premise was checked first and the framing was wrong (M-159).*** The four bytes cost **0.033 ms** to move; the `poll(Wait)` around them costs **0.375 ms of a 0.454 ms extraction** at 129³ because it drains every dispatch queued before it. So this ticket is not about a read-back, it is about a **synchronisation point** — and what that point waits for is the GPU doing work the caller asked for. |
| | | ***CPU time becomes independent of grid size: flat at ~0.17 ms from 33³ to 129³ (M-160).*** Against the synchronising path's 0.170 / 0.245 / **0.722**, i.e. **4.2× at 129³ and widening**. The flatness is the result rather than the ratio: with no wait, what remains is CPU *recording*, which does not depend on cell count. |
| | | ***The GPU still does the work, and the finding says so.*** A frame is no shorter; the CPU is free during it. Small for a demo extracting one surface, large for a caller meshing many chunks where stalls serialise what could overlap. Claiming a frame-time win here would have been M-149's overclaim a second time. |
| | | ***The allocation strategy is the ticket's real content, and it is a contract rather than a guess.*** Worst-case sizing is `MAX_TRIANGLES` = 12 per cell — **906 MB per buffer at 129³**, 1.8 GB for the pair, against 38,456 measured triangles, **190× wasted**. Grow-and-retry needs the total, which is the wait being removed. So the budget is the caller's; the emit shader stops at the buffer's own `arrayLength` rather than running past it; and `IndirectGeometry::total` carries the real count for a caller who chooses to check. Truncation is a contract with a detectable outcome, not a silent substitute. |
| | | *`arrayLength(&positions)` is what makes the bound free — no extra binding, no extra uniform, and the guard is derived from the thing it protects rather than passed alongside it and able to disagree.* |
| | | *E-303 shows the consequence honestly: its HUD reads **"actual: NOT READ BACK. hold [T] to pay for it"**, and holding `T` performs the explicit check. A demo that quietly displayed a number it could not have would have hidden the whole property.* |
| ☑ | **GPU-008b** | **`E-303 gpu_mesh_shader` — the Bevy example that actually draws with it.** | L | GPU-008a |
| | | ***The whole pipeline on the GPU, and the bus is never touched (M-158).*** Field evaluated, three moving brushes folded, Marching Cubes extracted with a GPU-scanned prefix sum, and the result drawn by a mesh shader straight out of the buffers the extraction wrote — **16,436 triangles at 0.41 ms, 65³, 60 fps**. Per frame the CPU sends a camera matrix and three brushes. No upload, no read-back, no vertex buffer, no index buffer. |
| | | ***Bevy 0.19 has no `Node` trait, and finding that out early is what made this tractable.*** `Core3d` is a **schedule** with `Prepass → MainPass → EarlyPostProcess → PostProcess` sets whose own docs invite extra systems, and `RenderContext` is a `SystemParam` handing out the command encoder. The draw is a system in `EarlyPostProcess`, which orders after the opaque pass by the chain rather than by naming a Bevy system and depending on it staying public. |
| | | ***A render pipeline must match the view's MSAA sample count, and wgpu refuses rather than degrading.*** *"The RenderPass uses textures with sample count 4 but the RenderPipeline uses attachments with format 1"* — Bevy defaults to 4×. `MeshShaderRenderer::new` gained a `samples` parameter, read from `ViewDepthTexture::texture.sample_count()` rather than assumed, because the view owns it. |
| | | ***The refusal branch is forced and screenshotted rather than asserted.*** `ISOMESH_NO_MESH_SHADER=1` makes the unsupported path reachable on hardware that supports the feature; without it, *"never panics on an unsupported adapter"* would be a claim nothing had ever executed. It draws nothing and says so. |
| | | ***The ticket's "shows the compute path" half is deliberately not built, and this is the reasoning.*** A vertex-buffer fallback inside this example would be a second execution path for one feature, and — worse — one that cannot be tested on any machine that can run the demo. `gpu_compute_mc` and `gpu_vs_cpu` **are** the compute-path demos; the HUD points at them by name instead. |
| ☑ | **GPU-011b** | **The general mechanism: a field a consumer supplies, evaluated on the GPU.** | L | GPU-011a |
| | | ***Chose the interpreted edit log over composing the consumer's WGSL, argued in writing on `FieldSampler`.*** Four reasons, in the order they mattered: it is what this crate's world already **is** (a base field plus an ordered op list — `BrushStack`, and `paint::Edit` after E-208); it **survives editing**, where composed WGSL would recompile a shader per carve, tolerable for a static CAD model and fatal for `game_dig`; it is **exactly testable** against `brush::apply` rather than against a second statement of the same field; and M-154 says the drift is in the expression rather than the GPU, so option (b) buys no accuracy. |
| | | ***Reproduces `BrushStack` to under `8.4e-7`, and the mesh is unchanged (M-157).*** Each op alone 1.19e-7, each shape alone 2.38e-7, a twelve-brush mixed log 8.34e-7, an empty log bit-identical to the base field, and a three-brush log at 49³ giving the same triangle count as the CPU-sampled equivalent. |
| | | ***The mixed-log test is the load-bearing one.*** Mixed adds and subtracts do not commute and a smooth add is not associative (M-36..M-38), so folding first-to-last is part of the value rather than an implementation detail — a shader that batched or reordered brushes for parallelism would pass every single-op test and fail only that one. |
| | | ***What it costs is on the type, not buried:*** the log is bounded by sphere, box and capsule with the three brush ops, so a consumer with an arbitrary analytic SDF is **not** served. Composing consumer WGSL stays the right answer for the CAD half of the audience and is additive — a second *feature*, not a second path to this one. |
| | | *The degenerate cases are matched rather than special-cased: a zero-length capsule is a sphere and a smooth-add of width zero is a plain `min`, both because `isomesh` says the degenerate case has a right answer, and both asserted on the GPU side.* |
| ☑ | **GPU-009** | **Mesh-shader kernels cannot be executed under test, and that is a coverage hole rather than a missing convenience.** | S | GPU-008a |
| | | ***Resolved by accepting the hole and documenting it, not by relaxing `unsafe_code` (M-156).*** The alternative would work and its entire `unsafe` body is `Self { enabled: true }` — but the stated goal is a crate that can be described, accurately, as safe Rust, and one `#[allow(unsafe_code)]` costs exactly that description for a test-only convenience. The decision is the user's, recorded rather than assumed. |
| | | ***`headless::Gpu` now says precisely what it does and does not cover***, as a table: mesh shaders **validate** headlessly (GPU-003's `naga` sweep needs no device), a mesh pipeline is **refused** headlessly (`MeshShaderRenderer::new` errors, asserted), and a mesh shader **never runs** here. Compute kernels are unaffected — Marching Cubes, the scan and the field sampler all run and are tested headlessly. |
| | | ***The claim is pinned by a test rather than left as prose.*** If wgpu stops gating experimental features behind an `unsafe` token, or the lint policy changes, the assertion fails and the table gets re-read instead of quietly becoming false. That is the same guard M-44 asks for, applied to a documentation claim. |
| ☑ | **GPU-011a** | **Evaluate the field on the GPU — prove the win before designing the general mechanism.** | M | GPU-010a |
| | | ***Predicted "under 2 ms and ≥4×" before writing it; measured 0.54 ms and 15.5× (M-155).*** The 129³ path goes from **8.37 ms to 0.54**, and from 2.4× to **37× ahead** of a single-threaded CPU. What was 86% of the run became nothing: the samples are produced where they are read and the bus is never touched. |
| | | ***The shape matters as much as the number: it is nearly flat.*** 0.22 ms at 17³ and 0.54 at 129³ — a **420×** increase in cells for 2.5× the time, the same not-remotely-saturated signature M-145 found in `count + emit`. The crossover moves to ~25³, on about 0.22 ms of fixed overhead. |
| | | ***Cumulative across GPU-010a, GPU-012 and this: 15.01 ms → 0.54 ms, 28× — and none of it from making the extractor faster.*** `count + emit` never moved: 0.11 ms then, 0.04 now. Every gain was data movement removed. |
| | | ***The deviation table is the other deliverable, and it falsified the hypothesis it was written to confirm (M-154).*** Nothing matches `libm` bit-for-bit — **not even the fields with no transcendentals** — because a GPU may contract `x*x + y*y + z*z` into fused multiply-adds. `box_exact` comes closest at 1 ULP because its expression is `abs`/`max`/`min` over a `sqrt` that is identically zero inside the box; `gyroid` matches on only 11% of samples. **So the drift is a property of the expression, not the GPU**, which is what GPU-011b has to design around. |
| | | ***And the consequence that matters is measured rather than argued: the mesh is unchanged.*** Identical triangle counts from CPU-uploaded and GPU-evaluated fields on all four, including `gyroid` at a non-power-of-two spacing — no sample within `7e-7` of zero crossed the sign test. |
| | | *One reporting correction on the way: the sampling pass cannot be timed on its own from the CPU, because `sample()` submits and returns — a clock around it reads 0.00 ms whatever the GPU does. | ☑ | **GPU-012** | **Write samples straight into a mapped buffer, for the consumer GPU-011 cannot help.** | S | GPU-001 |
| | | ***The approach the ticket named is a pessimisation, and the ticket is closed by measuring that rather than by shipping it (M-153).*** `mapped_at_creation` plus per-element `write_iter` costs **13.47 ms against 8.40** at 129³ — **1.6× worse**. wgpu says why on the type: `BufferViewMut` deliberately does not deref to `[u8]` because *"mapped memory may be write-combining memory"*, where a stream of small writes is far worse than one memcpy. | | | ***What did work was a copy the ticket had not named.*** `sampled` built a `Vec<f32>` and converted it to bytes in a **second full pass over 8.4 MB**. Evaluating straight into the byte layout takes the upload to **7.04 ms** and the whole path from **9.65 to 8.15 ms — 1.18×** — and from 1.95× to **2.33× ahead** of a single-threaded CPU. |
| | | ***The prediction was 43% of the upload; the answer was 16%.*** Half the target turned out to be an irreducible 8.4 MB memcpy. Registering the number before the work is what made that a visible miss rather than a quiet success — the change is still worth having, and the ticket's stated reasoning was half wrong. |
| | | *The acceptance asked for the saving to be measured rather than assumed, and it was: the negative result is the deliverable for two of the three variants. `FieldBuffer::uploaded` now carries the comparison table in its docs, so the next person does not retry the mapping.* |

| | | ***1.56× on the whole extraction path, and 14× on the stage it replaced (M-150).*** 129³ went **15.01 → 9.65 ms**, the prefix stage **5.24 → 0.37 ms**, and the path went from 1.27× to **1.95× ahead** of a single-threaded CPU. So mesh shaders are a fork in the shader pipeline rather than a flag on it. |
| | | ***Metal is still unmeasured, and the probe says so rather than inferring.*** This machine is Linux with a single Vulkan adapter. The test originally wrote a bare `@mesh` and called a `dispatchMeshWorkgroups` that does not exist — the task dispatch is a builtin **output**, `@builtin(mesh_task_size)`. | ☑ | **GPU-006** | `E-302 gpu_vs_cpu` — both live, timing HUD, resolution slider. Watch the gap **close** at small grids: launch overhead made visible. | M | GPU-005 |
| | | ***The ticket's expectation is confirmed and the crossover is about 40³.*** Warmed, median of three, against a single-threaded CPU: **17³ 4.4× behind, 33³ 1.4× behind, 49³ level, 129³ 1.27× ahead.** The gap does close at small grids, and it inverts. |

With the mesh left in GPU memory the ratio runs `1.04×` → **`0.01×`**: roughly **150× faster**, and the entire loss is data movement. |
| | | ***The ceiling is the upload, and it is architectural rather than a tuning problem.*** At 129³ upload is **8.61 of 15.03 ms (57%)**, and `FieldBuffer::sampled` evaluates the SDF **on the CPU** — so this design does not take field evaluation off the CPU's budget, it adds a copy to it. M-136 put field evaluation at 65–74% of the whole job on `fbm_terrain`, so on precisely the workload a GPU would help most, this design helps least. Evaluating the field in the shader is what would change it, and that is not this ticket. |
| | | ***It also falsified M-144's timing half, which this session had recorded an hour earlier.*** Those figures were each the *first* extraction in a process, so one call absorbed shader compilation, the first submit and driver init — **10.76 ms landing on a single `counts_readback`** against `0.04 ms` warm. The sweep now warms up twice before timing anything and takes a median of three, and M-144 carries the correction rather than losing the entry. |
| | | *`ExtractTimings` is returned from every `extract` rather than sitting behind a flag: a timing path that has to be opted into is a timing path nobody has run, which is how "the GPU is slower" gets reported without anyone knowing which part is. Adding it also removed `PartialEq` from `GpuMesh` — wall-clock in a derived equality makes two identical meshes compare unequal.* |
| | | *`ISOMESH_SWEEP=1` runs the sweep headlessly and writes `docs/measurements/gpu_vs_cpu.csv`, on `ISOMESH_AUTOCARVE`'s precedent: the product of this example is a table, and a table only obtainable by pressing a key cannot be regenerated from a command line.* |
| ☑ | **GPU-005** | `E-301 gpu_compute_mc` — asserts **bit-identical** to CPU, or documents the exact divergence. "Looks the same" is not an acceptance criterion. | M | GPU-004, B-003 |
