What is not stable is bit-identity — **93.8% at `h = 0.125`, 1.1% at `h = 0.1`, 98.0% at `h = 0.0625`** — because WGSL permits a multiply-add to be fused and this driver takes it, which is invisible wherever `h·i` is exact and near-universal wherever it is not. Quoting the bit-identical fraction without the spacing would be quoting a property of the fixture. |
| | | ***The ticket's own warning caught a real bug, on the first run.*** "Looks the same is not an acceptance criterion" — and the two meshes did look the same while **8,951 vertices were further than a ULP apart**. `GridParams::sample_position` used `mul_add` where `isomesh` uses `origin + h·i`, so the GPU was reading a field sampled at *different points*; `h = 0.125` makes the two forms bit-identical and had hidden it through all of GPU-004's suite (M-143). Fifth instance in this repo of an algebraic identity IEEE does not honour. |
| | | ***The guard is a crate test at a non-power-of-two spacing, and it asserts its own fixture can fail.*** At `h = 0.125` the two forms agree bit for bit, so a test written there cannot detect the defect — M-44's rule again. The new test runs at `h = 0.1`, asserts the offset rather than a ULP count, and separately asserts the spacing distinguishes the forms. |
| | | ***The device is Bevy's, which is the real test of GPU-001's API rule.*** The example reaches `RenderDevice::wgpu_device()` and hands it to a crate that has never heard of Bevy; `isomesh_gpu::headless` is not imported. `isomesh-gpu` is a **dev-dependency** of `bevy_isomesh`, on E-206b's precedent for `avian3d`, because the plugin's whole design is that a CPU-only consumer never compiles the renderer. Verified rather than assumed: the plugin's lockfile still holds exactly **one** `wgpu`, at 29.0.4, shared with Bevy. |
| | | ***The disagreement is drawn, not just counted.*** `V` colours the surface by classification through E-208's vertex-colour path, so at `h = 0.1` the sphere goes almost entirely amber — 98.9% rounding-only — which is a picture that says something a picture of two spheres cannot. |
| | | *Also measured, and it belongs to GPU-006: **the GPU is 5× to 25× slower here** — 11.54 / 11.95 / 14.36 ms against the CPU's 0.46 / 0.88 / 2.82 ms — because two blocking read-backs and pipeline setup dominate a grid this size.* |
| ☑ | **GPU-004** | Compute-shader Marching Cubes + readback. **Headless harness first, no Bevy in the room** — if it can't run against raw wgpu, the abstraction leaked. | L | GPU-003 |
| | | ***The case table is uploaded, not transcribed, so rule 5 is satisfied by having nothing to disagree with.*** The usual way a GPU port breaks "never guess a case table" is by pasting a second copy into WGSL. `case_table_bytes` packs `marching_cubes::table::CASES` — itself derived by a `const fn` rather than copied from a paper — and a test unpacks the bytes and compares all 256 entries field by field. |
| | | ***Two passes, because the output has to be dense **and** ordered.*** `count_cells` classifies and writes per-cell counts; the CPU prefix-sums them and sizes the output; `emit_cells` classifies again and writes at this cell's offset. An atomic bump allocator needs one pass and orders the output by whichever workgroup arrived first, which makes the result incomparable with anything — including itself. Cell order is the CPU's order, so the two line up by construction and a determinism test passes. |
| | | ***Bit-equality was expected, and the miss is the finding (M-142).*** Triangle counts match exactly on three fields. Of 6,936 vertices, **6,507 are bit-identical to a CPU vertex, 429 are within one ULP per axis, 0 are further** — worst separation `2⁻²⁴`. Both sides evaluate the same expressions on the same `f32` samples; WGSL permits a multiply-add to be contracted into a fused one and this driver takes it, rounding once where the CPU rounds twice. Diagnosed by measuring the magnitude rather than reasoning about it: 429 mismatches could have been a formula error and 1 ULP could not. The test asserts the bound, so two ULPs would fail it. |
| | | ***Normals are the one deliberate divergence, and the user chose it.*** A shader cannot call `Sdf::gradient`, so it central-differences a trilinear read of the sample grid at the cell size — the quantity `CentralDifference { step: h }` computes, whose distance from the analytic gradient M-65 already measured at 0.460° worst / 0.299° mean at 17³. Asserted against the analytic gradient directly rather than against the CPU's normals. |
| | | *Vertices are a soup: three per triangle, no cache, because the CPU's grid-edge cache has no cheap equivalent here. Welding is `isomesh::weld`'s job and runs on either. The kernel is registered as a `Composer` builtin, so GPU-003's naga sweep validates it with no GPU as well.* |
| ☑ | **GPU-003** | naga CI validation of every shader permutation. No GPU required. ~30 lines, highest value-per-line in the repo. | S | GPU-002 |
| | | ***"Every permutation" is `modules × 2^features`, and the count is asserted against what it is derived from.*** `Composer::variants` enumerates the cross product in a stable order — modules sorted, subsets in ascending bitmask order — so a failing sweep names the same variant every run. The sweep validates with the **same `naga` `wgpu` carries** (29.0.4, checked in the lockfile): validating with a different naga than the one that will compile the shader checks the wrong compiler. |
| | | ***M-44 applied twice, because a validation sweep is exactly the shape of gate it warns about.*** First: the variant count is asserted equal to `modules × 2^FEATURES` and non-empty, because a sweep over an accidentally empty set passes beautifully. Second: a companion test feeds the validator a call to an undeclared function and a line of prose and asserts it **rejects** both — a validator that accepted everything would keep the sweep green forever. |
| | | ***`FEATURES` is a `const` in the library rather than a literal in the test***, so adding an `#ifdef` to a shader without adding its symbol is the one way to hide a branch from validation, and it is visible in the source next to the shaders rather than buried in test scaffolding. It is empty today — `grid.wgsl` has no conditional compilation — and the cross-product machinery is asserted with *synthetic* features, because a combinatorial harness only ever run at n = 0 has not been run. |
| ☑ | **GPU-002** | Shader composition: `include_str!` + ~40-line `#include`/`#ifdef` preprocessor. **Not `naga_oil`** — see `CLAUDE.md`. | S | GPU-001 |
| | | ***Four directives and nothing else***: `#include <name>`, `#ifdef`/`#ifndef`, `#else`, `#endif`, over modules registered from `include_str!`. `naga_oil` stays out for the reason `architecture.md` §5 gives — it is Bevy-owned, was 14 months stale at review, and Bevy has said WESL is where it is going, so depending on it couples this crate to an engine's *outgoing* path. |
| | | ***`#include` pastes a module at most once, and that is forced rather than chosen.*** WGSL has no forward declarations and a duplicated function is a hard error, so "two modules both include the shared header" only works under include-once. |
| | | ***Which produced the bug worth recording (M-141): include-once and cycle detection interact, and the obvious ordering accepts a cycle silently.*** Checking *already included* before *currently inside* makes `a → b → a` skip and report success. The module docs already claimed a cycle is an error; the code did not implement it; the test asserting the doc is what caught it. Same commit, same cause, second instance: a bare `#ifdef` with no symbol missed the `"#ifdef "` matcher and was emitted as text, surfacing one line late at the orphaned `#endif`. |
| | | ***Shipped with a real module rather than a mechanism alone.*** `grid.wgsl` declares the `GridParams` layout the CPU side packs and the index/position helpers every kernel needs — including the multiply-don't-accumulate rule, with M-70 and M-73 cited in the WGSL itself. |
| | | ***And it is validated on a real device now, not deferred to GPU-003.*** "It is valid WGSL" is not a claim `contains` can make, so a test composes the module, wraps it in an entry point that calls **every** function so nothing is dead-stripped before checking, and runs it through `create_shader_module` inside a wgpu error scope. GPU-003 still owns the no-GPU `naga` sweep over every permutation, which is the version that belongs in CI. |
| ☑ | **GPU-001** | `isomesh-gpu` skeleton, `wgpu 29.0.3`. Public API takes `&wgpu::Device` / `&Queue` / `&mut CommandEncoder`. **Never a Bevy type.** | M | M-001a |
| | | ***"Skeleton" is taken to mean a complete small thing rather than a sketch of a large one***, because the no-stubs rule leaves no other reading. What shipped is the substrate a pipeline sits on and all of it works: a validated `GridParams` with the 32-byte two-`vec4` layout a shader reads, a `FieldBuffer` that puts any `isomesh::Sdf` into GPU memory, `read_buffer` to get results back, and a `headless::Gpu`. **Acceptance is a bit-for-bit round trip** — a sphere sampled on the CPU, uploaded, read back, and compared by bit pattern, on a real adapter. |
| | | ***The pin was verified rather than trusted, and it is one patch off what is written down (M-139).*** The root workspace and `bevy_isomesh` independently resolve **`wgpu 29.0.4`**, the same patch in both, from `"29.0.3"` caret requirements on both sides. So the pin does its job and the documented number is stale; what matters is that the two lockfiles agree. Five wgpu API details also differ from what a from-memory version would be, all caught by the compiler. |
| | | ***`headless` is in the library, not the test module, and that is a design position.*** GPU-004 requires the harness to run against raw wgpu with "no Bevy in the room", and a CAD tool has no renderer to borrow a device from — a GPU path reachable only through a game engine has already leaked. A Bevy consumer never touches it. |
| | | ***No software fallback, asserted by test.*** `force_fallback_adapter` stays `false` and a missing adapter is `Error::NoAdapter`. A test fails if `device_type` is `Cpu`, because a lavapipe run reports timings orders of magnitude off while looking merely slow, and the whole point of this crate is a comparison against the CPU path. The consequence — a machine with no GPU **fails** these tests rather than skipping them — is intended and is written on the module. |
| | | ***Measured while it was there (M-140):*** RTX 3090, Vulkan, `max_storage_buffer_binding_size` **2,147,483,644 bytes** and `max_compute_workgroups_per_dimension` **65,535**. At 4 bytes a sample that caps one binding at about **812³**, so GPU-004 will have to split a 1024³ grid — better known before writing the dispatch than after. |
| | | *No `bytemuck`, no `encase`, no `pollster`. The uniform is 32 bytes with no conditional padding, packed by hand; read-back needs the device polled rather than an executor; and the two futures wgpu does hand out are awaited by a 20-line `std::task::Wake` thread-park, which the workspace's `unsafe_code = "forbid"` made the only option and which turned out shorter than the raw vtable anyway.* |
| ☑ | **M-004** | **Write up M-001a..M-003** as `docs/research/YYYY-MM-DD-measured-comparison.md`. Numbers, method, hardware, and what surprised you. This is publishable on its own. | S | M-003 |
| | | ***Shipped as `docs/research/2026-08-13-measured-comparison.md`.*** Six algorithms, seven fields, two machines, from one codebase: topology, accuracy, speed, and the stage breakdown nobody publishes. Eighteen ledger entries are cited by number so every figure in it is traceable to the bench that produced it. |
| | | ***The synthesis produced a result none of the three tickets had on its own: four separate published figures in this project's sources are each one scene's number.*** The greedy-quads `2.76x` measures **1.70x–256x** (M-56); Nielson's `1.3%` manifold-split rate measures **0 on five of seven fields** (M-60); the `54%` contour share measures **13.1%–74.3%** (M-136); and ✗14's "cheapest in the family" is slower on every field on two machines (M-45). Four instances is a pattern rather than a coincidence, and the doc states it as a rule — *ask which field a mesher figure was measured on before believing it, including the ones in this document*. |
| | | ***Writing it also settled how to report the sweep.*** M-21's negative-intercept diagnostic and M-134's failure to reproduce its magnitude are contradictory only if the intercept is treated as a measurement; the doc adopts the amended form — **report the per-sample curve, not the intercept** — and says why the sign carried the meaning while the number never did. |
| | | *The per-field table was verified row by row against the committed CSV rather than transcribed, after M-002's caption was written wrong twice from numbers on its own screen. All 14 rows agree.* |
| ☑ | **M-003** | **`bench_stage_breakdown`** — stacked bar: contour / normals / weld / collider / upload. Published comparison: contouring 68 ms vs halfedge construction 58 ms — **the contour was 54% of a usable mesh.** Find your ratio before optimizing anything. | M | G-005, M-001a |
| | | ***The answer is 29%, against the 54% the ticket cites, and the biggest stage is not the contour (M-135).*** Mean share across seven fields at two resolutions: **contour 29.0%, weld 25.5%, collider 45.0%, normals 0.4%**. So optimising the extractor alone buys at most **1.41x** on the whole job — which is the ceiling this ticket existed to establish before anyone started. |
| | | ***And the single number is a fiction: the share runs 13.1% to 74.3% (M-136).*** `sphere` 13.1% at the low end, `fbm_terrain` **74.3%** at the high, with a 5.7x spread on identical code. The variable is how expensive the *field* is to sample — fBm noise swamps the fixed post-processing, a cheap analytic field does not. So "is it worth optimising the contour" has to be answered per workload, and the share rises with resolution everywhere because the contour is `O(n³)` while the post-passes scale with triangle count. |
| | | ***The fifth stage the ticket names has no bar, deliberately.*** Upload's CPU half is a **move** — `MeshBuilder` writes straight into the arrays a Bevy `Mesh` owns and `into_mesh` transfers them — so there is no pass to time; its GPU half needs a device the core crate must not have (rule 2) and belongs to GPU-001. A bar measuring a `Vec` move would imply a cost that is not there. |
| | | ***Two measurement traps avoided by construction.*** Every post-stage is timed from a fresh clone of the contour's output, because welding **mutates** and timing it twice on one buffer measures a second weld of an already-welded mesh. And the collider check runs on the **welded** mesh, because M-96 records that a validity measurement on unwelded output is meaningless and G-005's contract is welded to begin with. |
| | | ***One qualification on the headline, which survives it.*** What is timed is `collider::readiness`, which *validates*; a physics engine building its own structure is a different cost, and a shipping game may validate once rather than per chunk. But G-005 makes that contract the deliverable, and checking it is the largest line in the budget. |
| ☑ | **M-002** | **`bench_resolution_sweep` — the live plot only.** ~~fits `t = a + b·n³`, **prints `a`**~~ **The numeric half is done and its premise is falsified (M-62).** The bench had been printing `NaN` since the algorithm names were spelled out; fixed, and the answer is the opposite of what this ticket expected — Marching Cubes' `a` is **0.5118 ms, 0.64% of the largest run**, so there is *no* large fixed cost, and Surface Nets and Dual Contouring fit `a < 0`, meaning the model does not describe them. What is left is the **live plot**, which is a Bevy example and belongs in Phase 4. | S | T-006 |
| | | ***The plot exists because two coefficients cannot show a model failing.*** The bench already prints `a` and `b`; what it cannot show is that the two-term model describes one curve and not another. Drawn, the residuals are the picture — and the red `y = 0` axis is drawn separately so a fitted line crossing below it is visible *where* it crosses. |
| | | ***M-21 reproduces in direction, not magnitude, and that is the finding (M-134).*** Eight independent runs, median of three per point, 17³–89³: Surface Nets' `a` negative in **7 of 8**, Marching Cubes positive in 7 of 8, Dual Contouring positive in **8 of 8** — all under **0.18 ms** in absolute value against M-21's committed −3.13 ms over a sweep to 256³. So the sign carries the meaning and the value never did, exactly as M-21's own rule says. |
| | | ***An independent replication of M-62, which was not the plan.*** M-62 concluded from the committed CSV that there is no meaningful fixed cost. This reaches the same conclusion on a different machine, over a third of the range, with a different timing method. |
| | | ***Three drafts of the prose, two of them contradicting the numbers on the same screen.*** The first asserted "the dual methods dip below the red axis" and shipped a capture where *Marching Cubes* was the one below; the second over-corrected to "the sign flips between runs", which eight runs then showed was also wrong. Only counting the runs settled it. A demo whose caption argues with its own readout is worse than one with no caption. |
| | | ***And the chart rendered as three vertical lines at first.*** The harness builds its camera direction as `(cos yaw·cos pitch, sin pitch, sin yaw·cos pitch)`, so `yaw = 0` looks along **+X** and renders an XY chart edge-on. It needs `yaw = π/2`. |
| ☑ | **B-007** | **Does subgrid Marching Tetrahedra tile across a chunk boundary?** B-006 measured **1 open edge on `waves` and 0 on `blobs`**, which is neither, so `Extractor::Subgrid` reports `ChunkSeams::Unverified` rather than a guess. **M-79 names the mechanism this has to rule out:** subgrid's conformity is locality plus a *global* vertex ordering — two tetrahedra agree on the canonical `i < j` because they share the face's global indices — and M-79 warns in as many words that *"a mesh that renumbered vertices per tet would crack along every shared face"*. Chunks renumber. **The question is whether the ordering is derived from world position or from a chunk-local index**, which is a source question before it is a measurement one. Also rule out the weld: subgrid emits unwelded soup (M-93) and M-96 requires an *exact*-position weld, so a tolerance weld could be leaving the single edge rather than the extractor. **Acceptance:** the answer, the mechanism, and either `Closed` or `Gapped` in `chunk_seams` with the count pinned like the other three. | S | — |
| | | ***The answer is yes, and the source agrees with the measurement (M-132).*** 0 open edges in **20 configurations** — two fields, plus six field phases at three sampling resolutions each. `TETS[t]` is ordered by inclusion, so a tet edge always runs from the lower cube-corner index to the higher, which makes traversal a property of the grid. **M-79's warning is about a different renumbering**: it says a mesh renumbering vertices *per tet* would crack every shared face, and chunking renumbers per *chunk* while leaving the within-cell corner order alone. `chunk_seams` now returns `Closed` and the zero is pinned beside the other three. |
| | | ***The 1 that started this ticket was my own instrument, again.*** A chunk is 16 cells at 0.25 — **4.0 deep** — and the exclusion list gave `z` an upper bound of **8.0**, so the block's own `z` wall was never excluded and one of its edges landed in the seam plane. Tenth instance this session, and the **second in the same helper**: B-006 fixed it once for omitting `y` and left `z` wrong in the same edit. The bounds are now derived from the layout, so there is nothing left to write out — recorded as a Part 5 rule. |
| | | ***And it corrected a claim B-006 had already published (M-133).*** With the bounds right, `surface_nets` measures 5 on one field and **0** on the other, `dual_contouring` 4 and **1**. So the dual methods are *not reliably* seam-closing rather than *always* open — a weaker and more useful claim. `ChunkSeams::Gapped` now says that, because "leaves gaps at every chunk boundary" is falsified by its own second field and would have told a consumer the opposite of the truth on half their scenes. |
| ☑ | **E-208** | `game_paint` — spray colour on a wall, then blow a hole through it | Paint on the remaining wall is exactly where you sprayed it. Row 4 of the opportunities table. | G-003, B-002 |
| | | ***The research's own mechanism turned out to be unnecessary rather than cheap, and that is the finding (M-137).*** `novel-gameplay-opportunities.md:16` prices row 4 on **L²-nearest attribute transfer over a common subdivision** (Integer Coordinates §3.8, §4.4), which moves attributes from an old mesh to a new one — machinery you need exactly when the attributes live *on the mesh*. This crate's world is a base field plus an ordered edit log, so paint goes **in the log** and is a function of world position. A carve moves the surface; the paint was never on the surface. Measured drift across two carves that destroyed 20 probes' worth of surface: **exactly 0.000000**, where a transfer could only ever report a tolerance. |
| | | ***The first version of that measurement was worthless and looked perfect.*** The scripted run sprayed one colour, so repainting red over red was numerically identical to paint staying put: it printed `0.000000` at every step and would have printed the same on an implementation that smeared. Cycling the palette makes the same instrument sensitive — **27 of 40 sprays register drift, up to 0.886** — and only then does the zero across the carves mean anything. Earned a Part 5 rule: *an instrument that cannot report the failure has not reported the success*. |
| | | ***Two design questions were put to the user rather than assumed, and both answers shaped the module.*** Paint lives in the field rather than on the mesh, thin-shell rather than volumetric, in a new `crates/isomesh/src/paint.rs` that leaves `MeshBuffer` at three fields — so no extractor changed and T-007's 63 golden hashes keep their meaning. And a splat carries **no direction**: its shape is an arbitrary `Sdf` and already expresses whatever directionality a caller wants. The consequence is pinned by test rather than hidden — a shape that spans a thin wall paints the far face too. |
| | | ***The plan's `applied_after: usize` index did not survive contact.*** It pointed a splat at a prefix of the brush log, which needs a bounds check on a hot path and can be constructed wrong. Interleaving both edit kinds into **one ordered log** removes the failure mode entirely: when the walk reaches a `Spray`, the field value it is already carrying *is* the spray-time surface. No index, no error case, and O(log) instead of O(splats × log). |
| | | ***A spray dirties chunks without dirtying the field, which `mark_edit` correctly refuses to see.*** `PaintStack::sample` skips sprays without evaluating them, so the field either side of a spray is bit-identical and `mark_edit` answers "nothing moved" — right, and useless for finding what to re-shade. Carves take `game_dig`'s route; sprays name the nozzle's bounding box directly. Two routes to one dirty set, because two different things changed. |
| | | ***Cost of the exactness, measured (M-138):*** median ms per re-meshed chunk by log length — **0.1375 / 0.1748 / 0.2450 / 0.3200** over edits 1–10 / 11–20 / 21–30 / 31–40. **2.33x for 40x the log**, the same sub-linear shape E-202 measured for brushes alone. |
| ☑ | **E-209** | `game_csg_props` — place and boolean CSG primitives into the world live | Re-mesh per frame under moving primitives; concave sharp edges hold up. | G-003, A-007 |
| | | ***A concave edge, which is the case a CAD tool actually cuts, and the first one measured here.*** E-104 measures a **convex** corner — the easy case, whose solution is interior to its own cell. A block minus a quarter-space leaves a reflex dihedral whose position is known in closed form, so "how far is the nearest vertex" is a measurement rather than an impression. |
| | | ***And it moves, which is what makes the number honest (M-130).*** E-104 needed a rule about which resolutions to skip to defend one static configuration against grid alignment; a moving feature is unaligned almost everywhere and aligned occasionally, and only the worst over the sweep counts that. Mean / worst in cells: **dual contouring 0.0798 / 0.3481, surface nets 0.2895 / 0.5426, marching cubes 0.3412 / 0.5528** — decisively better typically, and converging toward the others at the extreme. |
| | | ***A prediction registered before the run, and falsified (M-131).*** The guess was that the cell clamp caps Dual Contouring here: M-28 found it costs nothing on a convex corner because the solution is interior to its cell, so a reflex edge whose solution "wants to sit outside" ought to be capped. Clamped and unclamped are **identical** — worst 0.3481 either way. The reasoning was wrong in a simple way: a reflex edge passing *through* a cell has its solution inside that cell too. M-28's result extends to concave features. |
| | | ***What does cap the worst case is left unestablished, on purpose.*** It coincides with the edge lying near a sample plane, which is E-104's alignment trap arriving continuously, but this demo does not isolate it — and the HUD says that rather than asserting a mechanism it did not test. |
| | | ***Not chunked, and the reason is now API.*** B-006 measured the dual methods leaving gaps at a chunk boundary, so a demo whose subject is Dual Contouring holding an edge must be a single grid. `Extractor::chunk_seams` says so at the call site for anyone reaching for the plugin instead. |
| ☑ | **B-006** | **`Extractor::DualContouring` and `Extractor::SurfaceNets` produce a cracked world on a chunked `VoxelVolume`, and the plugin says nothing (M-128).** Measured on two adjacent chunks: boundary edges in the shared plane are **0 for Marching Cubes, 5 for Surface Nets, 4 for Dual Contouring**. The mechanism is structural rather than a bug — a dual method's boundary quad needs the neighbour cell's vertex and a chunk does not have it — so this is not a fix to the extractors. **What is wrong is that the plugin offers the choice silently.** It shipped a cracked world into the README's lead GIF for one commit because the extractor had been switched during an unrelated investigation and nothing flagged it. **Acceptance:** either the plugin refuses the combination, or `VoxelVolume::with_extractor` documents it at the call site *and* a test pins the three seam counts so the numbers cannot drift. Do **not** "fix" it by welding across chunks — that is G-001's overlap doing a dual method's job and would hide the structural fact. | S | — |
| | | ***Reports rather than refuses, following the crate's own precedent.*** `collider::readiness` and `BrushOp::commutes_with` both make the truth available and leave the call to the consumer; refusing here would decide for someone meshing a single chunk, or whose gaps are never seen. `Extractor::chunk_seams()` returns `Closed`, `Gapped` or `Unverified`, and `with_extractor` says it at the call site — which is the thing that was missing, since saying nothing put a cracked world in this repository's own README for a commit. |
| | | ***Measured on two fields, not one, and the second one caught the instrument (M-129).*** The exclusion list for the block's own walls covered `x` and `z` but not `y`, so on a field extending past the chunk's vertical range the clipped top put one edge in the seam plane and **Marching Cubes was reported with 1 open edge on a seam it closes perfectly**. Ninth instance this session, and the *second time this exact omission* — E-205's crack counter needed the same fix four commits earlier. |
| | | ***Pinned as exact numbers, and mutation-tested.*** `the_seam_counts_are_pinned` holds 0 / 5 / 4 rather than `> 0`, per M-4's rule that a known defect with a number moves only when someone means it to. `chunk_seams_reports_what_was_measured` cross-checks the predicate against a live measurement; making it claim `Closed` for Surface Nets fails it. |
| | | ***Subgrid is `Unverified`, which is a state rather than a placeholder.*** It reads 1 and 0 across the two fields, and M-79 names a mechanism the measurement cannot rule out — its conformity rests on a *global* vertex ordering and warns that per-tet renumbering would crack every shared face, while chunks renumber. Split out as **B-007** rather than guessed. |
| ☑ | **E-207** | `game_editor` — sculpt with brushes, undo/redo over an op log | The CAD/editor use case. Undo is a re-fold of the log, not a snapshot. | G-003 |
| | | ***Undo is a re-fold, exactly as the ticket asked.*** The field *is* the log — `BrushStack { base, brushes: &log[..cursor] }` — and undo moves the cursor. Nothing is snapshotted, because a snapshot of a voxel world is the whole world while a log entry is a shape and an enum. The cost is the re-fold, **8.9 ms over 8 ops and 32 chunks**, and it grows with history because every field sample walks the whole log. |
| | | ***Round trip verified over a hash of the whole world, and it cannot pass vacuously.*** FNV-1a over every position and index, so one vertex moving by one bit anywhere fails it. The check also asserts the *undone* state differed from the original — without that, an undo that did nothing would pass. |
| | | ***`commutes_with` audited against reality on every adjacent pair, not one (M-126).*** Swap, re-fold, compare hashes, restore. Result: **5 said commute and the mesh was bit-identical, 2 said no and it moved, 0 unsound, 0 conservative** — the predicate is sound *and* tight on this fixture. It answers on ops alone and cannot see shapes, which is exactly where its conservatism lives: disjoint brushes commute whatever their ops, so the same audit on a disjoint fixture returns those 2 pairs as conservative. That refines M-37 rather than contradicting it — order matters across an add/subtract boundary **when the shapes overlap**. |
| | | ***The first fixture could not show the thing it was built to show (M-127).*** It moved each brush ~3 units between ops against radii of ~1, so consecutive brushes never touched and the audit reported **0 of 7 pairs where order mattered** — on a demo whose entire subject is that order matters. Fixed by tightening the path until consecutive centres sit inside the sum of the radii. Eighth instance this session of a fixture that could not exhibit its own property. |
| | | ***The audit runs itself at startup rather than waiting for a keypress,*** so a still carries both claims. One keypress would have checked one pair; this checks all of them, which is the difference between an anecdote and a measurement. |
| ☑ | **E-206** | `game_budget` — a deliberately overloaded edit queue under a frame budget | Frame time stays flat while the backlog drains. **Amortized cost per frame is the number no paper measures and the only one a game cares about.** | G-006 |
| | | ***The amortized figure, which is the ticket's whole point (M-124).*** 288 chunks under a swept budget, 2,360 frames each: mean cost per frame lands within 5% under the budget asked for at every setting above a few chunks — **1.905 ms against a 2 ms budget**, 6.269 against 8. Papers report throughput; a game cannot spend throughput, it gets 16.7 ms and then has to draw something. |
| | | ***The control is the only thing that makes a flat line worth reporting.*** The identical queue through `mesh_dirty` costs **20.62 ms in one frame** — missing a 16.7 ms frame outright — against a budgeted **peak of 2.10 ms**. That is a **9.8x lower peak for exactly the same total work**, which is the honest framing: a budget does not make meshing cheaper, it decides which frame pays. `U` runs it, and it also runs itself once at startup so a still carries the comparison. |
| | | ***G-006's never-livelock guarantee now has both its numbers (M-125).*** Its doc prices the after-the-fact predicate as "overshooting by at most one chunk". Measured, one chunk is ~0.072 ms and the worst overshoot is **0.099–0.172 ms across a 320x range of budgets** — bounded by one chunk and flat, as claimed. At a 25 us budget, a third of one chunk, the rate is **exactly 1.00 chunks per frame**: never zero, never two. |
| | | ***Two framing fixes found by looking at the capture.*** The queue now re-fills the instant it empties, because "deliberately overloaded" means the backlog never reaches zero — the first version only re-dirtied during a capture *sequence*, so a single screenshot photographed an idle world with every number reading 0.00. And the world grew from 128 chunks to 288 so the unbudgeted control misses a frame; at 128 it came in at 8.5 ms, a hitch nobody would notice, which would have undersold the comparison. |
| ☑ | **E-210** | `game_showcase` — **the demos prove correctness and undersell the crate.** Every gameplay demo runs on the same fBm *heightfield*, so nothing on that page shows a **cave, an overhang or an arch** — which is the entire reason to use an SDF voxel mesher instead of a heightmap. The pieces all exist and each is demonstrated alone; a game is all of them at once. **Wanted:** one hero demo on a field with real 3D topology, streamed, with a character carving through it under a frame budget, art-directed (a material worth looking at, lighting, no HUD) and captured with `ISOMESH_VIEW=nohud` as the README's top GIF, with the HUD stills kept beneath it as the evidence. **Acceptance:** a viewer who does not read the numbers can see something a heightmap cannot do. | M | E-205, E-206, G-003 |
| | | ***The field is the whole ticket.*** `solid(p) = max(p.y - height(x,z), |gyroid(p)| - t)` — a `max` is an intersection, so rock exists only where a point is below the surface *and* inside a thickened gyroid. The gyroid is triply periodic, so it tunnels in x, y **and** z, and the result has arches, connected caves and ceilings. Nine lines, nothing authored, and none of it has a height. |
| | | ***The gyroid's period is the entire look, and the first attempt got it wrong by 2.6x.*** At `K = 0.19` the period is 33 m — one tunnel is wider than the view and the world reads as a cracked plain. At `K = 0.5` it is 12.6 m and several arches are in frame at once. |
| | | ***The aliasing was the field's, not the extractor's (M-123).*** The rim where the two surfaces cross staircased; swapping Surface Nets for Dual Contouring changed **nothing**, which ruled the extractor out in one run. Fixed field-side: a smooth intersection rounds the knife edge wider than a cell, and the default shell moved from 0.55 to 0.85 — 2.2 cells to 3.4 — because a sheet has to clear the sampling limit like any other feature. This is M-72 arriving by composition rather than by resolution. |
| | | ***Art direction is part of the deliverable here, and it is stated as such.*** The harness's flat key light and charcoal background are right for reading a HUD off a mesh and wrong for a landscape, so this example replaces them with a sky, a warm key and a cool fill. Every other demo keeps the harness defaults. |
| | | ***Scope: carving is deliberately absent.*** `game_dig` and `game_destruction` already show it, and the acceptance was that a viewer who reads no numbers can see something a heightfield cannot do. A brush would not have moved that. |
| ☑ | **E-205** | `game_lod_flyover` — fly out and back across LOD transitions | No popping, no cracks, no hitching. Transvoxel doing its job at speed. | G-004, A-011b |
| | | ***No cracks, and the mirrored seam had never been run (M-120).*** E-107 meshes exactly one pair with the fine block on the low-`x` side, so `inset_boundary(face_bit(0, 1))` and transition cells at the fine block's *first* index are new here — the configuration most likely to have been silently inside-out, since no manifold or Euler check can see a mirrored winding. Across a full out-and-back: **0 open edges on both sides at every position**. |
| | | ***Proven by a control, not by the zero.*** With `ISOMESH_TRANSITIONS=0` the same worlds report **71 open edges low and 102–111 high**. The counter demonstrably reads failure, which is the only thing that makes its zero worth anything. |
| | | ***No popping is the wrong claim; the pop is 3.136 cells and now has a number (M-121).*** Meshing a block at both levels at the instant it switches gives a worst vertex displacement of **3.136 cells**, typically 0.6–1.6. A coarser mesh genuinely is a different surface, so the honest deliverable is the magnitude — which decides whether a fade can hide it — rather than a claim that it does not happen. |
| | | ***No hitching, but only once the naive version was fixed (M-122).*** Re-extracting all twelve blocks on any level change costs **12–23 ms** and misses the frame. Caching each block's *un-inset* extraction and rebuilding only the one or two that changed takes it to **4.6–12.4 ms**. Validation is a further 5–7 ms and is timed separately because it is this demo's instrument, not a cost a game pays. |
| | | ***The ladder runs along one axis, and that is stated rather than hidden.*** Every seam is an `x` seam, which is what makes the crack count unambiguous — there is no second axis for a failure to hide behind. A production terrain needs four faces, or six with caves, and this does not demonstrate that. |
| ☑ | **B-005** | **B-003's async tests do not drain on Linux, and one of them never passes (M-111).** Measured 2026-08-13 at `4369e3c`, a 12-core Ryzen 5900X on CachyOS, with no local changes to `bevy_isomesh/src`: `plugin::tests::spawning_the_work_does_not_do_the_work` fails **8 of 8** runs in isolation, draining **2 of 12** chunks in 500 `app.update()` calls, and the whole test returns in ~20 ms — so the updates are not blocking on anything, the spawned extractions simply never complete. The other three drain tests (`every_chunk_eventually_gets_a_mesh`, `an_edit_during_extraction_is_requeued_rather_than_swallowed`, `the_budget_bounds_what_lands_per_frame`) fail **intermittently** in the same suite, 1–3 failures per run across three consecutive runs. **This is B-003's acceptance property**, so the failure is either the property or the harness and it matters which. **First question, before any fix:** does the task pool have threads? `TaskPoolPlugin::default()` derives its counts from available parallelism, and `cargo test` runs the suite on N threads each building an `App` with its own pool — oversubscription is the obvious suspect and is *not yet checked*. **Acceptance:** the four tests pass 20 consecutive runs both in isolation and in the full suite, and the diagnosis is recorded — if the pool is the cause, say so; if the plugin only polls one task per frame, that is a plugin defect and not a test one. Do not "fix" it by raising the 500-update bound, which would hide whichever of the two it is. | M | — |
| | | ***The plugin was never at fault (M-118).*** The tests spun 500 `app.update()` calls with no yield, finishing them in ~20 ms against work needing ~49 ms. The probe that settled it moved wall time while *reducing* iterations: one millisecond of sleep per pass drained all 12 chunks in **39 iterations and 42 ms**. The pool ran the whole time — 2 chunks always completed — and `apply_finished_meshes` polls every task per frame, so both hypotheses the ticket named were ruled out before anything was written. |
| | | ***Five tests, not four.*** `the_subgrid_extractor_is_reachable_through_the_component` carried the same race and failed rarely enough to be missed in the first survey; it surfaced only at run 17 of 20. All five now wait on a **deadline** rather than an iteration count. |
| | | ***The obvious repair would have gutted the assertion (M-119).*** `spawn_cost * 4 < total` used the drain loop's wall time as the denominator, so any fix that let the loop wait would pad `total` with sleep and let a main-thread implementation pass — in the probe, 39 ms of the 42 ms total *was* sleep. The denominator is now the measured cost of one synchronous extraction, which takes the scheduler out of the assertion entirely. |
| | | ***Mutation-tested, per the ticket's own instruction not to fix this by raising the bound.*** Moving the extraction onto the main thread fails with *"the frame that queued twelve extractions took 149.18 ms, against 16.43 ms to perform ONE of them here"*. The reference extraction carries a negative control asserting it produced triangles, because a field that was free to sample would time no work and pass for any implementation. |
| | | ***Acceptance met as stated:*** **0 failures in 20 consecutive full-suite runs and 20 consecutive isolated runs.** A single green run would not have distinguished the fix from the coincidence the original was already relying on. |
| ☑ | **E-204** | `game_destruction` — shoot a wall, it craters, debris becomes rigid bodies | Runtime fragments are correct physics bodies, not pre-fractured props. Carve a spiral and a hollow shell — that's where decomposition fails. | G-005, G-003 |
| | | ***The debris is the boolean, not a prop.*** A shot appends `Brush::subtract(sphere)` to the solid's edit log — which craters it — and separately meshes the **intersection** of the solid-before-the-shot with that same sphere, which is exactly the volume removed. The crater and the fragment are two views of one operation, and nothing is authored. |
| | | ***Both shapes the ticket names are here, and the predicted ranking holds (M-116).*** A hollow shell's convex hull is a solid ball and a spiral's is a fat cylinder, so fragments get a decomposition rather than a hull. The shell needs the most pieces — **12.7 convex parts per fragment** against the wall's 9.2 and the spiral's 9.7 — and costs the most time. |
| | | ***The headline number is a cost, and it is bad: 241–272 ms per fragment, worst 369 ms.*** A 60 fps frame is 16.7 ms, so one fragment's decomposition is **14–22 whole frames**, charged on the frame the shot lands. Zero fragments failed to get a collider. So correct runtime destruction colliders are achievable and must not be synchronous — this belongs on a worker with the collider swapped in later, the way G-006 already treats meshing. |

An empty part now keeps its previous mesh and is hidden. Worth recording: the failure had no obvious connection to its cause. |
| | | *The camera sizes itself from the field's extent.* A fixed radius put it **inside** the gyroid, whose domain is several times the other fields'. |
| ☑ | **G-006** | **Frame-budget scheduler.** `mesh_within_budget(ms)` — process the dirty queue until a time budget is exhausted, resume next call. Priority by camera distance. | M | G-002 |
| | | ***The signature the ticket asked for is unimplementable, and the constraint improved it (M-78).*** `core` has no `Instant`, so a `no_std` crate cannot accept a millisecond budget without either a `std` feature — two paths, which this crate does not do — or a number it cannot compare against anything. `DirtySet::mesh_within_budget` therefore takes `spend: FnMut() -> bool` and the caller owns the clock: `\|\| start.elapsed() < budget`. |
| | | ***`spend` is consulted after each chunk, never before***, and that is a deliberate trade rather than an oversight. Checking first means a budget too small for a single chunk drains **nothing, forever**, while the queue grows — a livelock that presents as a memory leak. Overshooting by at most one chunk is the price. `a_budget_that_is_already_gone_still_meshes_one_chunk` pins it with a predicate that always returns `false`. |
| | | *Nearest-first by **squared** distance* — the ordering is identical and the square root is not free per chunk per frame — with ties broken by `ChunkId`, so the schedule is a pure function of the set's contents and the camera position and never of insertion order. `equidistant_chunks_break_ties_deterministically` inserts six equidistant chunks in six rotations and requires the same schedule from each. |
| | | *The unprocessed remainder goes back into the set in its own sorted order*, so a partial pass leaves `DirtySet` in exactly the state a fresh one would be. Every chunk is meshed once and only once across passes, asserted by draining a ten-chunk queue three at a time and comparing against `0..10`. |
| | | *The callback still receives `ChunkLayout::sample_origin`'s answer* rather than one the caller recomputed — the same guarantee `mesh_dirty` gives, for M-32's reason. |
| ☑ | **E-113** | `normal_estimation` — three panels, lit; differences live in the speculars, not the wireframe | — | A-012 |
| | | *The ticket's own phrasing turned out to be the design constraint.* The three meshes are **geometrically identical** — same positions, same indices, same 4,484 triangles — because `normals::recompute` overwrites the normal buffer and nothing else. A wireframe view of this comparison is three identical pictures, so the example ships with the wireframe off and a deliberately glossy material; a matte surface integrates away the very variation being compared. |
| | | ***The first capture was not worth shipping and was redone.*** It used `box_exact` on a cold grey, where **two of the three panels are byte-identical** — a box's analytic gradient is piecewise constant, so differencing a flat face returns it exactly and both read `0.000°`. A three-way comparison showing two identical pictures, with the meshes small in a frame a third empty. Reshot on `csg_difference`, where all three genuinely differ (**0.034 / 17.974 / 46.426** degrees worst), warm and framed to fill. |
| | | *Archived late — the row was left in `BACKLOG.md` when the work landed at `a0859e8`* and only found when a later survey printed it as open. The example, its screenshot and its README section were all committed at the time; only the checkbox lagged, which is exactly the failure the "check the box in the same commit" rule exists to prevent. |
| ☑ | **I-006** | **Amend CLAUDE.md where the 2026-08-14 review found it stale — with the user, not silently.** Two statements no longer match the repo they govern. (1) The version-pin table says wgpu "**29.0.3** exactly"; both lockfiles have been at 29.0.4 for the whole GPU series, and CLAUDE.md's own mesh-shader note already cites `wgpu-types 29.0.4`. (2) Rule 2's literal *"`grep -r "bevy" crates/` must return nothing"* is now enforced at the dependency-and-code level by the CI gate the review wrote, because 11 files of comments and docs legitimately mention Bevy when explaining the wgpu-follows-Bevy pin — the rule's intent (no Bevy dependency, no Bevy in code) survives; its letter does not. This file's own "Definition of done" repeats the grep form and moves in the same commit. CLAUDE.md is the user's rule file: propose the exact wording, get a yes, then edit. | S | — |
| | | ***The wgpu row was not a stale number, it was the wrong kind of claim.*** The obvious fix — swap `29.0.3` for `29.0.4` — replaces one false exact claim with another. Bevy 0.19 requires `29`, Cargo resolves the newest compatible patch, and that is why both lockfiles moved on their own without anyone bumping anything. The row now reads **29.x — whatever Bevy 0.19's requirement resolves to**, with the observed 29.0.4 in parentheses as an observation rather than a pin. The rule underneath it — *never bump `wgpu` or `glam` independently of Bevy* — is unchanged, and is the part that was always doing the work. `## Layout`'s second copy of the same number was corrected with it. |
| | | ***Rule 2 kept its intent and lost its letter, which is the whole reason this needed a yes.*** The literal `grep -r "bevy" crates/` had been red since GPU-001 against 11 files that are all comments, READMEs or manifest notes *explaining why wgpu follows Bevy's pin* — the rule's own reason, cited as a breach of it. The rule now states the checkable thing: no dependency, no code, prose permitted, with CI checking comment-stripped manifests and non-comment `.rs`, and `cargo metadata` as the strong form. Verified at 0/0/0 on all three. |
| | | *Measured while writing it, and it turned up a fourth version claim nobody was tracking.* The root lockfile carries **glam 0.33.3** where the pin table says 0.32 — legitimately, via `parry3d` as a dev-dependency, and `crates/isomesh` depends on no glam at all (`cargo tree -e normal` is `isomesh + libm`). The table governs the Bevy-facing workspace, where glam is 0.32.1 as claimed. Left as-is rather than widened: this ticket named two statements and changing a third silently is the habit it exists to correct. |
| ☑ | **A-016** | **QEF accumulation order breaks rotation equivariance for cells with 3+ crossings.** Found by the 2026-08-14 review and deliberately left report-only, because the fix moves golden hashes and a re-baseline is not a decision a review makes silently. `dual_contouring/solve.rs` sums the six-entry normal matrix, `g`, and the mass-point centroid in raw edge-label order; a lattice rotation permutes the labels and float addition is not associative, so extract-then-rotate and rotate-then-extract differ in last bits wherever a cell carries three or more crossings — the bit-exact equivariance ✗12 and the adjacent-math audit record as delivered ("0/9600 sorted") holds only where the sorting was actually applied. The same file already carries the remedy for its determinant: `sum_equivariant` sorts by magnitude before summing, and its own comment calls the unsorted effect "a real bug." The existing equivariance test maps crossings to *unrotated* edge labels and structurally cannot see this. **Fix (the review's verified sketch):** accumulate per-component 12-slot arrays keyed by edge label and reduce each through `sum_equivariant`, in both the QEF accumulation loop and `HermiteCell::centroid`; add a test that permutes the edge visit order and asserts bit-identity. **The real work is the cost, which is why the review stopped:** all 42 DC/MDC golden rows change bit-wise. Re-bless with `ISOMESH_BLESS` and read the diff — positions should move by last-ulp amounts only, and any change to a vertex or triangle *count* means the fix is wrong. | S | — |
| | | ***The review's sketch was insufficient, and the test that proved it was written before the fix.*** The ticket said to reduce twelve-slot arrays through `sum_equivariant`, which is right as far as it goes and does not go far enough: `sum_equivariant` sorted on the *absolute* values alone, so a `±c` pair tied and a stable sort resolved it by arrival order — the very labelling a rotation permutes (**M-175**). Its doc comment had asserted the opposite (*"ties need no tie-break"*) since ✗12, and survived 9600 measured trials because `dot_equivariant`'s terms are products of two components, where a signed permutation flips both signs and the terms permute without negating. The tie-break now compares the signed values. |
| | | ***And the property the ticket named is not fully reachable — the bound is now on record rather than assumed (M-177).*** `φ(−S) = −φ(S)` cannot be had from any ordering rule: a magnitude tie group holds `m` copies of `−c` and `n` of `+c`, negation swaps the counts, and no multiset-derived order maps a group onto its own reverse unless `m == n`. Since a lattice rotation can negate a component, a sum of *components* is not bit-exactly equivariant under the full octahedral group by this route; the three-term dot product is, for M-175's reason. **What is delivered is the weaker and sufficient property**: the vertex is a function of the crossings and not of the edge labels they arrived under, tested by `the_vertex_does_not_depend_on_which_edges_the_crossings_arrived_on` with a negative control beside it. Anything stronger needs exact summation, not a better sort. |
| | | ***Cost: 34 of 168 golden rows, not the 42 predicted, and 0 count changes (M-178).*** The four that held are `box_exact` and `thin_plate` at 17³ and 33³ — the axis-aligned fields at the two resolutions whose cell size is a binary fraction (16 and 32 cells; 25³'s 24 is not), where the coordinates are exact, the sums are exact, and reordering cannot move a result that was never rounded. Both fields *did* move at 25³. Verified after blessing: same 168-row set, 34 hashes changed, **0 vertex or triangle counts changed**, no algorithm outside `dual_contouring` and `manifold_dual_contouring` touched. |
| | | *Two corrections to the ticket's own text, made while doing it.* `HermiteCell` is in `hermite.rs`, not `dual.rs`. And the reductions moved to a new crate-internal `equivariant` module rather than being exported from `dual_contouring::solve` — `hermite` is the *input* type to the solve, so importing from it would have inverted the layering. `transvoxel/cell.rs:231` carries a structurally identical centroid, recorded here and deliberately not widened into this ticket. |
| ☑ | **T-009** | **One weld epsilon, not three.** The 2026-08-14 review found the tolerance scattered and left it that way deliberately: `validate::WELD_EPSILON_REL` is the documented `1e-4` relative to cell size, `weld.rs`'s own doc example welds at an *absolute* `1e-6`, and call sites across the extractors' tests pass `cell * 1e-6`. Normalizing shifts pinned non-manifold censuses — which is exactly why this is a ticket rather than a drive-by. Pick the one policy (the documented relative constant is the candidate; `weld.rs:92`'s own comment argues the absolute form is a trap at small `h`), route every weld through it, and re-pin every census that moves, reading each moved count rather than blessing blind. | S | — |
| | | ***The ticket said three epsilons; there were four, across 38 call sites.*** `h · WELD_EPSILON_REL` (1e-4) at 11, `cell_size * 1e-4` written as a bare literal at 1 (`collider/tests.rs`), `cell * 1e-6` at 9, and `h * 1e-5` at 5 — that last one in the benches, the plugin's tests and two Bevy examples, i.e. the places nobody greps. The remaining 12 are absolute (`1e-9`, `1.0`, and the doc example's `1e-6`) and all of them are in `weld/tests.rs`, on hand-built unit-scale fixtures with no `h` at all. Those are **left alone deliberately**: they test the welder as a geometric primitive at a chosen tolerance, which is not the same act as applying a policy. Only the doc example was wrong, and it was the worst one, being the crate's front door. |
| | | ***And the ticket's premise was wrong (M-181): normalising moved nothing.*** *"Normalizing shifts pinned non-manifold censuses — which is exactly why this is a ticket rather than a drive-by."* It shifts none of them. Welding the subgrid output at every factor from `h·10⁻⁹` to `h·10⁻³` gives the **same vertex count at all seven** on every field. The plateau's edge was then measured because it is the half that is actually useful: the first factor that changes anything is `h·10⁻²` on `gyroid` and `fbm_terrain`, and `h·10⁻¹` on four more, while `box_exact` never changes even at `h/2`. **The policy therefore sits 100× below the nearest tolerance that changes an answer, and 1000× below on five of seven fields.** |
| | | ***That margin is the reason the scatter was survivable and the reason it was dangerous.*** Four numbers spanning two orders of magnitude agreed on every field this crate tests, so nothing ever failed and nobody ever looked. They stop agreeing the moment a field puts two genuine vertices `10⁻⁵·h` apart, and the disagreement would then appear as a census drift in whichever test happened to use the odd one out. |
| | | *Enforcement is a function rather than a convention.* `weld::epsilon_for(cell_size)` is the one expression, documented with the `2²⁴ · epsilon` extent argument that rules the absolute form out, and pinned against `ValidateConfig::weld_epsilon()` by `the_one_policy_is_one_number_however_it_is_reached` — because if the welder and the validator ever disagree about what coincident means, the validator's duplicate count is describing a different mesh than the welder produced. |
| | | *Bonus: this closes **P-7 limb (a)**, which asked for the margin and not just the outcome.* `thin_plate` holds at 422 vertices across the whole plateau and first moves at `h·10⁻¹` — 1000× the policy — where it collapses to 128, the through-thickness merge P-7 was worried about, finally happening. Recorded on P-7's own entry. |
| ☑ | **A-014h** | **Subgrid MT — an endpoint root's identity, and the geometry question underneath it.** M-169's remedy, re-scoped on measurement rather than attempted as written. **The stated mechanism is falsified (M-179):** *"a crossing at parameter 0 or 1"* describes nothing — of 52,488 roots across the seven fields at 17³, **none reports `t == 0`** and 36 report `t == 1`, all on `gyroid`. `refine` returns the *upper* end of its final bracket by design, so a root at an edge's lower endpoint comes back as a tiny positive parameter. The positions are asymmetric too: `a + (b − a)·t` **never** rounds onto `corners[a]`, and lands exactly on `corners[b]` 1,692 times on `box_exact`. **And the ticket's acceptance is unreachable as written (M-180).** Raw → distinct-by-bit-pattern → welded: `box_exact` **1262 → 1028 → 338**, `csg_difference` **1280 → 1094 → 482**, `sphere` **830 → 830 → 812**, `gyroid` **4020 → 4014 → 4014**. The middle column is the ceiling on *any* rule that leaves positions where the extractor put them, so an exact identity removes 234 of `box_exact`'s 924 duplicates and cannot touch the other 690 — they are positions IEEE calls different numbers, because two tetrahedra reach one grid point along different edges and the lerp rounds differently on each. **The split is per field and runs both ways**: `gyroid`'s 6 merges are entirely exact (an identity closes it), `sphere`'s 18 are entirely inexact (an identity closes none of it). **What the ticket now is:** decide whether the extractor may canonicalise an endpoint root onto the grid point's own position — `origin + cell_size · index`, the expression `corners` already uses, which every cell computes identically (M-32). That closes the gap completely and is arguably *more* correct, since it removes a rounding artifact rather than adding one. It is also a **geometry change of a few ulps on every affected vertex**, which is a decision about output and not about keying. Both instruments are committed and pin every number above. **Acceptance, once the decision is made:** `how_complete_the_shared_table_is_against_a_positional_weld` reports the on-lattice residue at 0 on all seven fields; the 21 subgrid golden rows re-blessed deliberately with **the triangle set unchanged**; and `how_much_of_the_positional_weld_an_exact_identity_could_ever_reach` re-pinned to show raw == welded. | M | — |
| | | ***Both of the ticket's own premises were falsified before a line of it was written, and the second one re-scoped it.*** M-179: `t == 0` describes nothing — 0 of 52,488 roots report it, because `refine` returns the *upper* bracket end by design. M-180: an exact identity tops out at 1028 of `box_exact`'s 1262 vertices where the acceptance wants 338, because 690 of the duplicates are positions IEEE calls different numbers. A partial implementation matching the original shape was written, measured, and reverted rather than kept. |
| | | ***The remedy is placement, not keying, and that was the user's call.*** An endpoint root is emitted **at the grid point's own position** — `origin + cell_size · index`, the expression `corners` already uses and every cell computes identically (M-32) — instead of at the lerp's answer, which differs in the last bits between the edges meeting there. Detection is `f(G) == 0` **exactly**, no tolerance: that is the definition of the surface passing through `G`, and it is a property of `G` alone, so all up-to-24 tetrahedra meeting there agree without consulting each other. |
| | | ***Result: the identity is complete on every field (M-184).*** Raw → welded is now an identity on all seven — `sphere` 812 → 812, `box_exact` 338 → 338, `csg_difference` 482 → 482, `thin_plate` 422 → 422, `gyroid` 4014 → 4014 — and each of those raw counts is exactly what the positional weld used to reach. **The cost's *shape* is the check that it is right**: 15 of 168 golden rows, 14,171 vertices removed and 28,342 triangles — **exactly 2 per vertex on all 15 rows independently**, which is the edge-collapse signature `(ΔV, ΔE, ΔF) = (−1, −3, −2)` and preserves χ. The 6 subgrid rows that held are `torus` and `fbm_terrain` at all three resolutions: the two fields M-169 measured with **zero** vertices on a grid point. |
| | | ***One behavioural change it forced, and it is a fix (M-185).*** Merging two roots that sit on one grid point turns the sliver between them into a triangle with two equal indices, which `validate_indexed` calls a structural error rather than a recorded metric — 36 on `sphere` alone. The extractor now declines to emit them. That is not dropping geometry: they carried no area before the merge either. **Every pinned defect census is unchanged** — `csg_difference` (3, 6, 6), `gyroid` (0, 0, 138), `fbm_terrain` (4, 6, 19) — so a change that moved 15 golden rows disturbed none of the numbers those rows exist to protect. |
| | | ***And it unblocked A-014d by falsifying M-162 (M-186).*** M-162 was the measurement that blocked A-014d: 33 coincident polygons on `csg_difference`, 27 with foreign tetrahedra on their boundary edges, 150 triangles cross-cell. Now: **3 coincident, 0 foreign edge users, 0 cross-cell, all within one cell** — and `box_exact`, which M-161 called the field with the most coincidence of all, is at **0**. Most of what M-162 measured was never §3.2.3's immersion; it was one grid point wearing many names. |
| | | *A third thing fell out unlooked-for (M-186's sibling).* `raising_the_sampling_changes_the_topology_it_finds_but_not_by_much_else` had recorded that its first version *asserted bit-equality and failed*, because bisection converges to *an* ulp of the root and which one depends on the bracket. At 16 against 32 samples the two now agree **bit-for-bit on all 1,984 vertices**, so the test asserts what it originally wanted to. |
| ☑ | **A-014f** | **Subgrid MT — the orientation vote has no consistency guarantee between neighbours.** Split out of A-014d on measurement, not on taste: A-014d claimed `gyroid`'s `(0, 0, 138)` as *"the same cause wearing a different face"* as §3.2.3's immersion, and M-164 falsified that. `gyroid` has **1 coincident polygon in 8,088 triangles**, so there is essentially nothing there to inset. Of the **186 triangles standing on the 138 inconsistently-oriented edges: 0 are zero-area, 15 have the gradient lying in the triangle's own plane, and 171 have a decisive vote.** So for 92% of them A-014e's `dot(face_normal, gradient(centroid))` returned an unambiguous answer on *both* sides of the edge and the two answers disagreed — two triangles of one sheet, each correctly oriented against the gradient at its own centroid, wound opposite ways. **The tension this ticket has to resolve is A-014e's own rationale.** The vote is per triangle rather than per patch deliberately, because a sheet thinner than a cell puts two oppositely-facing surfaces inside one tetrahedron and a per-patch vote would flip one of them wrongly — that reasoning is sound and `thin_plate` is the case it protects. But a *local* answer that is right at every triangle is still not a *consistent* orientation across the mesh, and `gyroid` is where the difference shows: a triply-periodic surface passes close to itself, so the nearest sheet to a centroid is not always the sheet the triangle is on. The obvious remedy — orient one connected component from its most confident triangle and propagate — is a design choice with a real cost (it needs connectivity before orientation, which the per-tetrahedron soup does not have until it is welded) and it is **not** to be adopted without deciding that trade deliberately. **Acceptance:** `gyroid`'s inconsistently-oriented count driven to 0 in `the_validity_suite_over_every_reference_field` **without** regressing `thin_plate`, whose two sheets 0.4 cells apart are the reason the vote is per triangle in the first place; and the 15 in-plane-gradient triangles given a defined answer rather than an arbitrary one, since a vote of exactly zero currently falls to the `else` branch and keeps §3.2's own winding. | M | A-014e |
| | | ***The design choice was settled by a prediction, not by taste — which is what P-7 was registered for (M-182).*** The ticket said the remedy *"is not to be adopted without deciding that trade deliberately"*, and the trade was whether propagation could invert one of `thin_plate`'s two sheets. P-7 had already named its own falsifiers; measured, welded `thin_plate` is **one component, 0 boundary edges, χ = 2, 0 non-manifold, at 17³/25³/33³**, and the component count holds at every epsilon from `h·10⁻⁹` to `h·10⁻²`. One closed genus-0 surface joined at a rim is one component *by topology rather than by tolerance*, which is precisely the precondition under which propagation is safe. |
| | | ***And the concern turned out to point the other way.*** M-182 found `thin_plate` itself incoherently wound at the two resolutions the suite never tests — `17³ → 0, 25³ → 8, 33³ → 6` — so A-014e's per-triangle vote was already failing on the field it was written to protect. There was a coherent orientation to find and the local rule was not finding it. |
| | | ***Result (M-187): zero on every mesh whose edges are all manifold, and the law holds on all 21 rows.*** `gyroid` **138 → 0, 150 → 0, 330 → 0**; `thin_plate` **8 → 0, 6 → 0**; `csg_difference` **36 → 0** at 33³. Where a residue survives the mesh has non-manifold edges, and an edge carrying four faces cannot have them pairwise oppose — no assignment of windings fixes it, so that residue is A-014d's defect wearing an orientation costume rather than a limit of propagation. Vertex counts, face counts, manifoldness and boundary counts are asserted unchanged across the pass. |
| | | ***Placement: a post-weld pass the caller composes, not a rule inside the extractor.*** The ticket named the cost — *"it needs connectivity before orientation, which the per-tetrahedron soup does not have until it is welded"* — and that sentence is the answer. `orient::orient` runs after `weld` as `weld` runs after `extract`, works for any extractor rather than this one, and leaves A-014e's per-triangle vote in place as the *seed* rather than replacing it. One path, two stages: the vote says which way out is, propagation makes the answer global. |
| | | *The second acceptance clause, the 15 in-plane-gradient triangles, is answered structurally rather than by a tie-break.* Seeds are taken in **descending confidence**, so a triangle whose gradient lies in its own plane is reached last and inherits an orientation from a neighbour instead of inventing one — with `the_seed_is_the_most_confident_triangle_not_the_first` as the test that seeding by index would have failed. A Möbius band is **reported** rather than silently half-fixed (`non_orientable_components`), because propagation returning with the opposite sign is a real answer and picking one of the two arbitrarily is not. |
| | | *Found while measuring, and it re-scopes A-014d (M-188).* Non-manifold edges by resolution: `csg_difference` **3 at 17³, 0 at 25³, 0 at 33³**; `torus` **0, 4, 0**. So the `(3, 6, 6)` row A-014d exists to drive to zero is a **17³-only** defect, and `torus` — believed clean — is non-manifold at a resolution nothing tests. T-010 is now the ticket the whole A-014 series is waiting on. |
| ☑ | **T-010** | **The subgrid validity suite tests one resolution where Phase 1's gate says three.** Found by P-7's measurement, not by a review (M-182). `the_validity_suite_over_every_reference_field` sets `let n = 17u32` and stops there, while this phase's own header requires *"no unexplained violations on all seven test fields **at three resolutions**"* — the other extractors are swept at 17/25/33 by the golden harness, and the subgrid entry's census is not. **The cost is already known and is not hypothetical:** `thin_plate` reports 0 inconsistently-oriented edges at 17³ and **8 at 25³, 6 at 33³**, so the one resolution that is tested is the one where the field A-014f exists to protect happens to be clean. Widen the sweep to `[17, 25, 33]`, then **read every count that appears rather than pinning it blind** — the other six fields are unmeasured at two of three resolutions and may carry their own. Expect the pinned table to grow from 7 rows to 21, and expect some of the new rows to be non-zero: a known defect with a pinned number and a ticket that owns it satisfies this gate, an unexplained one does not. | S | — |
