| ☑ | **D-012** | L | |
> **DONE 2026-08-24 — the site is live at `isomesh.ladvien.com`, nine demos are playable in it, and the front page runs `isomesh` itself.** The ticket did not exist when the work started, which is the first thing worth recording: the first half shipped as **PR #2** — eight rendered pages, three playable modules, a `site` job that builds on every PR and a `pages` job that deploys `main` — before anything named it. This row is the whole of it, created and archived together, because `BACKLOG.md`'s rule 2 is one ticket one commit and retro-fitting a ticket to describe half-finished work would have been a fiction.
>
> **The roster grew from three demos to nine, plus one module that is not a Bevy build at all.** Six examples took the same two edits and nothing else: `canvas: Some("#isomesh-canvas".into())` in their existing `Window` literal, and `use bevy::platform::time::Instant` in place of `std`'s. `quickstart` was the exception and stayed the exception — it is the one example with no `WindowPlugin`, because its whole point is being the shortest path, so it gained the six lines that bind the canvas and nothing more. `game_dig`'s and `game_showcase`'s `std::env::var` overrides return `Err` in a browser and each degrades to its own documented default; that is stated on the page rather than worked around, because a URL choosing a stream radius is not a feature anyone asked for.
>
> **`plugin.rs`'s clock was a defect in the published crate, not demo plumbing (✗44).** `use std::time::{Duration, Instant}` at `:51` and `Instant::now()` at `:395` inside `apply_finished_meshes`, which is to say inside the one system every streaming consumer runs. It compiles on `wasm32-unknown-unknown` and panics there, so `IsomeshPlugin` was unusable in any browser and took `quickstart`, `game_showcase`, `game_terrain_stream`, `game_walk` and `game_capsule_walk` with it. The fix is `bevy_platform = "0.19"` with its `web` feature on wasm only, and it is verified rather than assumed: `cargo tree --target wasm32-unknown-unknown -e normal -i web-time` puts `web-time v1.1.0` in the **normal** graph under `bevy_isomesh`, and the same command without the target prints "nothing to print", so native carries no `web-time` and changes no behaviour. `Duration` stayed on `std` throughout — it carries no clock.
>
> **`isomesh_web` is a third workspace, and `-Oz` was measured and rejected (M-362).** 133,115 bytes, zero wasm imports, sixteen `extern "C"` exports over linear memory, no `wasm-bindgen`: `MeshBuffer`'s fields are `pub` precisely so a consumer can read them without a copy, and hand-written `Float32Array` views are both smaller and fewer moving parts than bindgen glue. `#[unsafe(no_mangle)]` is the crate's only `unsafe` token and `build_web.sh` refuses to build if an `unsafe` block, `fn`, `impl` or `trait` appears in it — a checkable rule where the `unsafe_code` lint cannot draw one, since edition 2024 has no way to emit a wasm export without that attribute. `wasm-opt -Oz` was then tried on it and **rejected on its own number**: 133,115 → 112,642 bytes raw but 49,837 → **50,493 gzipped**, so 15.4% smaller on disk and 1.3% *bigger* on the wire, which is what a front page actually pays. The same ≥15%-gzipped rule rejected it on the Bevy modules, so `binaryen` is in neither the build nor CI.
>
> **Four gates, each demonstrated failing before being left passing.** `doc_facts.sh` derives `PLAYABLE` from the `DEMOS=(…)` array in `build_web.sh` — the one place that decides what is built — and holds four things against it: the "nine of the demos are playable" claim in `web/index.md` and `bevy_isomesh/DEMOS.md`, `play.html`'s allow-list count, and the existence of a `#notes-<name>` block per allow-listed demo. **The plan's own phrasing would have shipped a gate that could never fire:** it specified "**Nine** of the demos are playable", and `check()` matches `(word|digits)[ -]+phrase`, which `Nine**` does not satisfy — the bold markers sit between the number and the phrase. Caught by deliberately breaking the count and watching the gate stay green. The prose is de-bolded and the gate now bites in both files. Adding `web/index.md` to `DOCS` also turned out **not** to bring its "seven extractors sit behind one trait" claim under the existing extractor check, since neither existing phrase matches it; that claim now has its own check.
>
> **`preflight.sh` gained the site.** `build_site.py` is the repository's link checker — every relative `](target)` in nine markdown sources resolved against the real filesystem, plus a per-page heading-count equality that is the only check able to see markdown being swallowed — and it ran only in CI, so the gate most likely to break on a docs edit was the one a local run could not see. It is now a fast-set step, along with `isomesh_web`'s own fmt, clippy and test, which `cargo clippy --workspace` structurally cannot reach. E-111's lesson, applied to a third workspace.
>
>
> **Two things measured on the flagship that are not this repository's to fix, and are stated rather than
> filtered.** `game_showcase` streams 411 chunks and 229,580 triangles and renders correctly, and while it
> does, Bevy 0.19's `bevy_render` slab allocator logs *"Use-after-free: attempted to copy element data for
> an unallocated key"* — **3,186 times in a 45-second run**, starting the moment mesh churn begins, with no
> other error of any kind and no panic. It is upstream, it does not stop the demo, and it is documented on
> `play.html` instead of being silenced, because a log filter that hides a renderer's errors hides the next
> real one too. Separately, **the plan's "below 20 FPS" contingency could not be evaluated here**: this
> machine is a Wayland session with no reachable X authority, so Chrome runs headless on SwiftShader and
> every demo reports the same 250 ms/frame — `shifted_linear_root`, which draws two charts, reports it as
> well. That number is the software rasteriser's ceiling and discriminates nothing, so no wasm-only budget
> constants were added on the strength of it.
> **Every claim on the page was verified in a real browser rather than in CI**, because nothing in CI can see a canvas. All nine demos: exactly one `<canvas>` (two would mean a demo failed to bind `#isomesh-canvas` and Bevy appended its own), `#loading` removed, non-black pixels counted from a clipped screenshot decoded through `createImageBitmap`, and the three Phase 21 cross-checks read out of the console — `8 fields x 9 columns of p-57.csv`, `all 13 reproduce`, `12 of 12 quoted numbers agree`, zero mismatches. Two instruments in the plan were wrong and neither was the site's fault: `readPixels` on a re-fetched context returns zeros because the drawing buffer is gone after compositing and `preserveDrawingBuffer` is ignored on an existing context, and `page.on('console')` never fires through this harness at all — a `console.log` probe produced no event — so console capture goes through an `evaluateOnNewDocument` shim instead.
>
> **Two things about the deployment that are worth knowing before the next one.** The repository had **never** deployed, so the TLS certificate is provisioned only after the first successful `pages` job — `https://` refuses the connection until then, while `http://` already answers 200, and both `README.md` and `DEMOS.md` link the `https://` form. **It is slower than an hour**: measured at more than two hours after a successful deploy, `gh api repos/Ladvien/isomesh/pages` still carried `status: null`, no `https_certificate` key at all, and `protected_domain_state: null`, with the site serving 200 over `http://` the whole time. Re-saving the custom domain through the API did not change it. GitHub's own bound is 24 hours, not an hour, and the CAA chain was checked and is not the cause — `isomesh.ladvien.com` CNAMEs to `ladvien.github.io`, whose CAA set permits `letsencrypt.org` and `digicert.com`. So `https_enforced` is deliberately still `false`: setting it before the certificate exists takes the site down rather than securing it. The one command that finishes this, once `https://isomesh.ladvien.com/` answers, is `gh api -X PUT repos/Ladvien/isomesh/pages -F https_enforced=true`. Rewriting the `README.md` and `DEMOS.md` links to `http://` was considered and rejected: that ships a permanent downgrade to dodge a transient wait. And GitHub's API refuses a `mergePullRequest` that touches `.github/workflows/` without the `workflow` OAuth scope, which this token lacks; the merges went through `git merge --no-ff` and a push over SSH instead, preserving the same history the plan asked for. PR #5 also turned out to be **stacked on PR #4's branch** rather than on `main`, so merging it landed on `ci-actions-node24` and #4 became the carrier for both — #4 was never "subsumed" by #5, it was the other way round by the time it mattered.

| ☑ | **GPU-014** | M | GPU-011b, E-202 |
> **DONE 2026-08-25 — `game_dig`'s eighth key evaluates its field on the device, and the gate is a byte count rather than a stopwatch.** The demo was running the configuration `isomesh-gpu`'s own docs call the wrong one: `FieldBuffer::sampled` per chunk per edit, which `lib.rs:73-76` prices at **87% of the path** and which *"does not take field evaluation off the CPU's budget; it adds a copy to it"*. It could not do otherwise — `field.wgsl`'s base was a `switch` over four hard-coded reference fields and `Ground` is a hand-rolled sinusoidal height field — so `FieldSampler` gained `fold_into`, the same kernel with the base taken out of the shader's vocabulary and read from a buffer the caller filled. `Ground` is a pure function of position, so a chunk's base samples are correct for the life of the process and only the *log* moves: **19.6 KB of samples per re-mesh becomes 64 bytes per surviving brush.**
>
> ***The acceptance is an equality on bytes, not a ratio on a clock.*** After the key-8 switch drains, `GpuFields::sample_bytes` is **5,030,912** — `EXTENT`'s 256 chunks × 17³ samples × 4 bytes, one base each — and a carve that re-meshes chunks already meshed adds **zero**. Both are asserted in `key_eight_meshes_the_sandbox_on_the_gpu`. M-304's sibling lesson is why: a wall-clock assertion on a machine with a governor swings tens of percent run to run and cannot gate anything, while a byte count is exact and machine-independent. The measured split is *reported* and has no threshold — release build on this host's RTX 3090, cache miss `base 0.038 ms / fold 0.038 / count 0.027 / scan 1.697 / emit 0.018`, cache hit `base 0.000 / fold 0.042 / count 0.019 / scan 0.058 / emit 0.018`. **These are from the committed tree**, re-read after the last code change rather than quoted from an earlier build — a table citing a run that no longer exists is how two published figures in this repository ended up describing runs absent from the files they cite.
>
> ***The plan's primary binding scheme is a validation error, and the contingency was the only path (V-47).*** It proposed binding `sample_stack`'s own output buffer at binding 4 on the paths that never read it, "free" because one kernel keeps one layout. It is not free: `STORAGE_READ_WRITE` is in `wgpu_types::BufferUses::EXCLUSIVE` (`buffer.rs:146`) and wgpu-core's `invalid_resource_state` (`track/mod.rs:331`) rejects any exclusive state with more than one bit set, so a buffer bound `read_write` at 2 and `read_only` at 4 in one bind group fails `merge_bind_group` — and the check is in wgpu-core, so a native Vulkan run does not get away with what WebGPU would reject. Read before written rather than found by a failing test: a 4-byte `unused_base` created once in `FieldSampler::new` fills the slot instead, and the layout stays single — **one kernel, one pipeline, two public entry points over one private `run`.**
>
> ***Two doc comments were falsified by this change and are corrected rather than left.*** `gpu_dispatch` claimed *"all eight keys pay the same field cost against the same pruned tape, and what differs is the extraction"*, and `drain_dirty`'s pruning comment said the same. After this they deliberately differ — seven keys walk the survivors per sample on the CPU, the eighth uploads the log and folds it on the device — and that difference *is* the finding key `8` now demonstrates. The HUD prints `base … (cached)` and `fold` separately, plus the honest verdict: at 17³ the CPU wins (0.06 ms against 0.22, M-296), because a fixed setup cost cannot amortise over 4,913 samples. **A 17³ chunk is the one row in that table where the GPU loses**, and a demo that offered a GPU key while saying nothing invited the opposite assumption.
>
> ***Nothing here ports a second extractor to the GPU, and the reason is measured.*** `count + emit` is **0.045 ms at 129³** and flat across a 420× rise in cell count (M-296), so a second GPU extractor would optimise a term that is already neither large nor growing. `docs/research/2026-08-11-meshing-speed-analysis.md:110-113` ranks per-cell arithmetic fourth at 1.5–3.9×, which is *the entire spread between extraction algorithms* — rank 4 of 4. Media Molecule's production note that Dual Contouring is the easy one on a GPU (fixed one-vertex-per-cell output) and this repo's own `emit_quads` wall at **IPC 0.72 against 3.83** both say the dual methods would port well; they would port well into a term that is not the cost.
>
> ***Verification.*** `fold_into` against `BrushStack` over an uploaded non-`GpuField` base: worst deviation **4.77e-7** over 25³ samples with an add, a subtract and a smooth add in the log, inside M-157's 8.4e-7 for edit-log agreement; an empty fold is the base **bit for bit**; a base of a different sample count returns `SampleCountMismatch`. `isomesh-gpu` 13/13 `field_sampler` tests, `game_dig` 14/14 — including `the_gpu_mesher_agrees_with_the_cpu_one` **unchanged**, which after this change is the stronger claim of the two: the CPU walks `BrushStack` per sample while the GPU folds the same log in a compute shader, so the triangle counts agreeing means the two field paths agree.

| ☑ | **D-013** | L | E-202, D-012 |
> **DONE 2026-08-26 — `game_dig` reads as terrain rather than concrete, the sandbox is visibly walled, the player is a person, a phone can play it, and the site serves over HTTPS at last.** Created and archived together, the D-012 pattern and for the same reason: the work was user-directed and rule 2 is one ticket one commit, so retro-fitting a ticket to describe finished work would be a fiction. Five changes and two questions that needed investigation rather than code.
>
> **Two of the asks were already answered and the answers are the deliverable.** *"Are the collision meshes kept up to date"* — **there are none.** Collision samples the SDF directly every frame, and `game_dig.rs:246-255` already states why: the demo edits the field on every frame of a held button, so a collider cache is invalid before it is built (M-116 puts a convex decomposition at 241-272 ms per fragment). Nothing can go stale, so the stuck-in-holes complaint was never staleness — the mechanism is below. *"Is the player a human-size capsule"* — **it was neither.** Two spheres of `BODY_RADIUS = 0.4` at `[0.4, 1.2]`: 1.6 tall and **0.8 wide**, human height at 2.7× human width, and the spheres touched at exactly one point (separation `0.8 = 0.4 + 0.4`) so the "capsule" had a pinched waist a lip of rock could pass through. Now four spheres of `0.25` at `[0.25, 0.65, 1.05, 1.45]`: **1.70 × 0.50**, spacing `0.40` against a `0.50` diameter so they overlap, and narrower than the `0.50` cavity the default brush carves — the player can follow their own tunnel. `the_body_is_a_capsule_that_never_hangs_above_the_terrain` asserts the shape as an identity on the constants rather than as an outcome of a fall.
>
> **The terrain is a four-layer texture array, and the layer index is the one thing a renderer cannot report (✗45's sibling risk).** `Grass002`, `Ground023`, `Ground051` and `ground_0046` packed into two `512x2048` PNGs, four maps each, by the recipe `textures/PROVENANCE.md` already carried. `triplanar.wgsl`'s bindings 101 and 103 became `texture_2d_array<f32>` and `Triplanar.settings.z` stopped being unused: negative blends grass by slope (`smoothstep` on `n.y`, 0.55–0.82) and dirt by depth (`smoothstep` on world `y`, −1.6 to −0.4), non-negative forces one layer. The walls are a **second `TerrainMaterial` instance**, not a second material type — the layer is a uniform, so both share one pipeline and one pair of arrays and differ in 16 bytes. `lw` needs no renormalising: `up·s + (1−up)·s + (1−s) ≡ 1`. **18 texture samples per fragment where there were 6**, unconditional, because a per-fragment branch around `textureSample` is a WGSL validation error; the branch that exists is on a uniform, so both arms are uniform control flow.
>
> **512² per layer rather than 1024², measured rather than assumed:** four layers at 1024² is 16 MB of decoded RGBA per image on the main thread at startup against 4 MB, and at 1.5 world units per tile a 512 tile still puts **341 texels on a world unit** — more than a 993×558 canvas resolves at any distance the player stands. Layer 3's alpha statistics come out within **2e-6** of the previous single-set 1024² pack (`0.738711/0.885381` against `0.738713/0.885382`), which is the downscale doing nothing but resampling and is the cheapest available proof that the same bytes landed in the new slot. `PROVENANCE.md` now tables alpha mean and σ **per layer**, because a single flat layer averages away against three good ones.
>
> ***The silent failure in a stacked array is the stack order, and no test that renders can see it.*** `reinterpret_stacked_2d_as_array` slices top-down and validates only that the height divides, so a stack built bottom-up divides perfectly and paints the boundary walls with grass. Two gates instead: `the_terrain_array_is_four_square_layers_stacked_top_down` asserts `512`, `height == 4·width`, `depth_or_array_layers == 1` before and `4` after, on the committed bytes and through the same `terrain_array` function `setup` calls; and `the_shader_and_this_file_agree_about_the_texture_array` reads `triplanar.wgsl` with `include_str!` and holds its `@binding(101)`/`@binding(103)` declarations and its three layer constants against this file's, because a `texture_2d<f32>` against `dimension = "2d_array"` is a pipeline-creation failure at the first draw and a swapped index is no failure at all.
>
> **The sandbox is walled, and the walls are the boundary rather than a picture of one.** `sandbox()` returned the editable box and only `aim`/`trace` ever consumed it, so the boundary existed and nothing on screen said where it was. `walls()` derives five slabs from it and `WALL_THICKNESS`, each occupying exactly the thickness immediately *outside* one face — flush with the boundary plane, disjoint from the interior, so nothing z-fights the terrain and `aim`, which refuses to carve outside `sandbox`, can never dig them. Side walls overhang by one thickness in the other horizontal axis so the four vertical corners close. No ceiling, because fly mode exists to leave the box and look down into it; the floor is not decoration — `Ground` is solid to `−inf` while chunks stop at `y = −5.4`, so a shaft dug to the bottom reaches a depth where the mesh ends and collision does not, and the slab is what the player sees there. `move_camera` clamps `x`/`z` to `lo + BODY_RADIUS … hi − BODY_RADIUS` in **walk mode only**.
>
> ***The wall test is a geometry test and a material test, and the second one is why it runs `setup` in full.*** `five_slabs_line_the_sandbox_from_outside_with_the_corners_closed` asserts each slab is thin in exactly one axis, disjoint from the sandbox, flush with a face, spanning the full height, and overhung by `2·WALL_THICKNESS` — properties against `sandbox()`, so an `EXTENT` change moves both sides. That still cannot see the failure that matters: `material` and `wall_material` are both `Handle<TerrainMaterial>` and differ by one word, so handing the walls the wrong one compiles, spawns five entities, and renders grass on the boundary. `setup_dresses_the_five_walls_in_the_concrete_layer` runs the real `setup` under `RunSystemOnce` — `init_gizmo_group` only touches resources, so no `GizmoPlugin` and no renderer are needed — reads the material asset back and asserts `settings.z == LAYER_CONCRETE` and `!= ` the `SurfaceMaterial` handle. **Mutation-tested**: injecting `MeshMaterial3d(material.clone())` fails it with the intended message.
>
> ***The stuck-in-holes bug, and the fix that was wrong in the other direction (✗45 / M-363).*** Three causes. The one that trapped the player was a **single-point ground probe**: pressed against the wall of its own pit the body has rock under part of its foot sphere and air under the centre, one sample read air, `grounded` went false and `world.grounded && Space` refused the jump. The specified fix — five samples in a flat cross at the same depth — hovers the body **12.5 cm** off flat ground, because the lateral samples reach into terrain higher than the terrain under the foot; the settle test caught it on the first run at `1.8249992` against `1.70`. What ships is the **chord**: lateral samples at `0.7·R` sit `sqrt(R² − r²)` below the centre and only then drop by `GROUND_PROBE`, so the tolerance is purely vertical. Scanned over 108 pit fixtures: **34** where one point reads airborne and the chord cross reads grounded; bare-terrain hover over five columns is `0.0000130 … 0.0419`, every one inside `GROUND_PROBE = 0.06`. Cause 2 was `contact = Some(n)` **inside** the resolve loop, so velocity cancelled against whichever sphere came last in `BODY_OFFSETS` rather than the deepest overlap — a body with its head in a lip and its feet on the floor cancelled against the head. Cause 3 is arity: two spheres to four needs `RESOLVE_PASSES` 2 → 3, twelve sample-and-gradient pairs a frame against eight.
>
> **A phone can play it, and touch feeds the same intent the keyboard does rather than a second control path.** `TouchIntent` carries a look delta, a walk axis, three buttons and a sticky `seen`; `touch_input` classifies each touch **by where it started** (left 40% is the stick, the rest is look) so a drag never changes role mid-gesture, and `move_camera`/`dig` read those numbers beside the keys. `touch_axes` is a free function with a dead zone, a unit-**disc** clamp so a corner drag is not `√2` faster than a straight one, and a negated `y` because screen `y` grows downward. Two details that are not incidental: look accumulates `Touch::delta()` rather than displacement from the start, which means **a thumb parked on `DIG` contributes exactly zero rotation** and the buttons need no exclusion rectangle; and `JUMP` is edge-triggered off `Ref<Interaction>::is_changed()`, legitimate because `bevy_ui`'s focus system assigns `Pressed` only on the transition (`focus.rs:310`), so a resting thumb does not bunny-hop where `Space` held does not. `web/style.css` gains `touch-action: none` on the canvas — without it the browser claims the stream for scroll and the stick receives one `touchstart` and nothing after.
>
> ***The `grabbed` flag had to stop meaning two things.*** `dig`'s edit gate requires `world.grabbed`, which on a desktop is set by the first left click and *also* drives `CursorGrabMode::Locked`. A phone has no pointer to lock and iOS Safari does not implement the API, so `touch_input` sets `grabbed` on the first touch and `grab`'s lock arm is gated on `!touch.seen` — one flag, two consumers, and only the cursor one excluded.
>
> **HTTPS, which is what the *"does not work on Safari mobile or Chrome"* report actually was (✗46 / M-366).** Not browser-specific and not mobile-specific: **WebGPU is secure-context-only**, the site was reachable only over `http://` because `isomesh.ladvien.com`'s certificate never provisioned, and `https://ladvien.github.io/isomesh/` **301'd to the http custom domain** — so `navigator.gpu` was `undefined` and `play.html`'s gate fired in *every* browser, desktop Chrome included. The custom domain is gone and the `CNAME` write is out of `build_web.sh` **first**, in that order, because a `CNAME` file in the artifact re-sets the domain on every deploy. Clearing it was not sufficient: `Ladvien/ladvien.github.io`, an archived 2022 fork, still carried Pages custom domain `ladvien.com`, and an **account-level** domain rewrites every project page on the account — to a host served by AWS S3 + CloudFront that GitHub provably does not serve, which 404'd. `gh api -X PUT` on an archived repo returns **409 `Repository is archived.`**, so it took PATCH `archived=false` → PUT `{"cname": null}` → PATCH `archived=true`. `ladvien.com` is unaffected. The site now returns **200 with `num_redirects=0`** at `https://ladvien.github.io/isomesh/` under GitHub's own valid `*.github.io` certificate, `https_enforced: true`.
>
> ***The gate on that page was passing on a browser that cannot render, and it was found live rather than reasoned about.*** `play.html:247` tested only that the `navigator.gpu` **object** exists. On the deployed site, in Playwright Chromium 1228 headless here, `typeof navigator.gpu` is `object` and `await requestAdapter()` is **`null`** — so the gate passed, `overlay.remove()` ran, `await init()` resolved because `spawn_app` returns immediately on wasm, Bevy failed asynchronously inside the event loop, and the page was a **black canvas with no message**. This also falsifies the deploy skill's own claim that headless Chrome here exposes no `navigator.gpu`. The gate now awaits the adapter and names the two cases apart; verified by serving the patched `web/dist` locally on the same browser and reading `#loading` back.
>
> ***A landing state, because `play.html` had none.*** `URLSearchParams.get("demo")` returns **`null`** when the key is absent, and that was folded into the unknown-demo branch, so opening `play.html` printed `unknown demo: null` — a null nobody typed, over a black canvas, under an irrelevant orbit-camera key list, with no link to any demo. No link in the repository produces a bare `play.html`; the URL that does is the one this repository's own docs quote for verification. Three states now: absent renders an index of the nine, unknown renders an accurate message *and* the index, valid is unchanged. Built from the `DEMOS` allow-list with `createElement` and `textContent` — never the query string, never `innerHTML` — so the file's existing claim that nothing on the page is built from user input stays literally true, and verified with `?demo=<img src=x onerror=…>`: `imgs: 0`, `window.__pwned` undefined. The count in the prose is `Object.keys(DEMOS).length`.
>
> ***A latent test deadlock, tripped by the four tests this ticket added (M-365).*** `wgpu_pair()` opened a `wgpu::Instance` per call — five at sixteen tests, and green. At twenty-three the suite hangs: reproduced at `--test-threads` 12 and 24, clean at 8 and 16, so a race and not a threshold, and the first sighting cost 1800 s against a `timeout`. The six device-opening tests run alone pass in 1.35 s and the fifteen others in 1.33 s, so no single test looks guilty. `wgpu::Device` and `wgpu::Queue` are `Arc` handles, so one `LazyLock` cloned per test is both the fix and what an application does. **23/23 green over five consecutive default-parallelism runs, and 1.5 s → 0.6 s.**
>
> ***Verification, and what could not be verified here.*** `cargo test --example game_dig` **23/23**, five consecutive parallel runs. `backlog_gate.sh`, `doc_facts.sh` (*"9 playable demos — no drift"*) and `preflight.sh --full` (**23 ok steps, all green**) clean. Live HTTPS confirmed 200/no-redirect on four entry points; the new gate's message and `touch-action: none` confirmed in Chromium against a locally served `web/dist`; the wall-material gate mutation-tested. **The rendered image was not verified on this host and that is stated rather than skipped**: `big` has no reachable display (`XDG_SESSION_TYPE=tty`, sddm's X is root-auth'd, `XOpenDisplayFailed`), Xvfb is not installed and sudo needs a password, so grass on the flat tops, leafy dirt on the slopes, deep dirt in a fresh tunnel and concrete on the five walls still need `cargo run --release --example game_dig` on a machine with a display — as do the three touch buttons on an iPhone. The mechanical substitutes for the parts a screenshot would have shown are the array-shape and shader-agreement tests above.

| ☑ | **D-014** | M | D-013 |
> **DONE 2026-08-26 — the floor and the walls stop the player, the frame rate and the mesher survive a hidden panel, and the GPU job cap bounds something for the first time.** Two user-reported defects, and a third found while gating the first.
>
> **1. Digging out the bottom dropped the player through the floor (✗48 / M-369).** D-013's own reasoning was *"no `y` clamp — the field already stops descent, and the floor wall is scenery"*, and it was wrong about where the field stops. `aim` refuses a brush *centred* outside `sandbox`, but a brush centred **on** the floor plane reaches its own radius below it — up to `2.00` against a floor at `y = -5.4` — so a shaft dug to the bottom removes the field under the box while the chunks and their meshes stop at `-5.4`. The body left the visible world and fell until it met whatever `Ground` still had, embedded in rock nobody can see or dig.
>
> ***A `y` clamp could not have fixed it, and that is the more useful half.*** Clamping a coordinate stops the fall without ever reporting ground, so the body hangs at the limit with `grounded` false, the jump refused, and `gravity_step` accelerating into a surface it is not allowed to touch. The boundary has to be in the **field** the resolver samples, so depenetration, the ground probe and the jump all see it — which is also what deleted D-013's `x`/`z` clamp, a second path doing the same job worse. `Walls` is five half-spaces built from the same `sandbox()` call `walls()` draws from, unioned with the brush stack **for collision only**: the mesher must not see them, because they are already on screen as five cuboids and meshing them would draw them twice. Half-spaces rather than the 0.5-thick slabs deliberately — the body only ever touches an inner face, and a half-space has no far side for one fast frame to tunnel through.
>
> ***No collision meshes, and that is the same answer D-013 gave.*** The ask was for collision *meshes*; what shipped is collision, without them. This demo's entire premise is that it has no collider cache because it edits the field on every frame of a held button (M-116: 241–272 ms per fragment for a convex decomposition), so adding a mesh collider for the walls would be a second collision system in the one demo built to prove none is needed. The outcome asked for — you cannot sink through the floor — is delivered by the field.
>
> **2. The frame rate went with the panel.** `update_hud`'s `!flags.hud` branch printed only the hint, so `game_dig` — which *opens* with the panel hidden — showed the mesher on its banner and nothing about cost. The frame rate now joins the hint line, which is the `[H] HUD` line the ask named; it goes on the hint rather than the banner because the banner already carries the mesher, so neither is duplicated when the panel is open. `nohud` still yields an **empty** frame, which the nine committed GIFs depend on, and that is asserted rather than assumed.
>
> **3. `GPU_JOBS_MAX` bounded nothing (✗47 / M-367), found by the load the D-013 tests added.** The cap was the second clause of `mesh_within_budget`'s budget predicate — and that predicate is consulted **after** meshing a chunk, deliberately, so a budget too small for one chunk still makes progress. So a frame beginning at the cap dispatched one more regardless, and since that repeats on every frame in which no readback retires, the queue grew by one per frame with **no bound**: 17 at frame 39, then **18** once the assertion was loosened to `cap + 1`. Two fixes that look right were tried and measured wrong first: *refusing to enter the drain while full* stalls the switch outright (a frame that dispatches nothing defers every chunk, and the overshoot was what had been carrying the drain), and *asserting `cap + 1`* is the first term of a series rather than a bound. What holds is checking `in_flight` **before** `gpu_dispatch` and putting the refused chunk back in the dirty set — deferred, not dropped, and not quietly meshed on the CPU either, because key `8` means the device meshed it.
>
> ***And the cap was being compared against a stale number (M-368).*** `drain_dirty` seeded its in-flight count from `pending.jobs.len()` while running **before** `gpu_collect` in the same chained frame, so it counted every job the device had already finished and nothing had retired. With the cap enforced and the order left alone, a 256-chunk switch collapsed to **one chunk per twelve frames** with the queue pinned at 16 for 900 frames. `gpu_collect` now runs first: retire, then dispatch into the freed slots. A job dispatched this frame is still collected next frame at the earliest, so the latency is unchanged and the suite went back under a second.
>
> ***The 900-frame guard was an assumption about the machine, and it is gone.*** It was chosen when this drain took 135 frames on an idle device, and a red *"the GPU switch never drained"* about a correctly-working demo is what a fixed count buys. Every drain-wait in the test module now goes through one `drain` helper that asserts the job cap on every frame and gates **progress** rather than speed: the outstanding count must decrease within 1200 frames, which is machine-independent because a live device always retires something. Six fixed loops became one helper.
>
> ***Verification.*** `cargo test --example game_dig` **26/26 over eight consecutive default-parallelism runs**. Both fixes mutation-tested: removing `Walls` from `move_camera`'s union walks the body to `x = 42.1` and fails `walking_into_the_wall_stops_at_the_sandbox`, which gates the *wiring* while `the_collision_boundary_is_solid_exactly_where_the_slabs_are` gates the *field* on all five faces and `digging_through_the_floor_lands_the_body_on_it_rather_than_losing_it` reproduces the reported bug and its fixture's premise. `a_hidden_panel_still_shows_the_frame_rate_and_the_mesher` drives `update_hud` itself — the `!flags.hud` early return is the branch that dropped the number, and a test that formatted its own line would not cross it — and checks the `nohud` empty frame in the same test, because the two requirements pull opposite ways. `preflight --full` **23 ok, all green**. The rendered image is still unverified on this host for D-013's reasons: no display, no Xvfb, no passwordless sudo.
